Figure 5
.
{\mathscr{L}}_{ul}
operational Semantics

The semantics is unsurprising:
The top frame’s continuation (
{\sigma.\mbox{{\texttt{cont}}}}
) contains the statement to be executed next.
We dynamically enforce a simple form of module-wide privacy:
Fields may be read or written only if they belong to an object (here
y
) whose class comes from the same module as the class of the object
reading or writing the fields (
this
).
9
9

9

More fine-grained privacy,
e.g.
C++ private fields or ownership types, would provide all
the guarantees needed in our work.
Wlog, to simplify some proofs we require, as in Kotlin, that method bodies do not assign to formal parameters.

Private methods may be called only if the class of the callee (the object whose method is being called – here
y_{0}
) comes from the same module as the class of the caller (here
this
).
Public methods may always be called.
When a method is called, a new frame is pushed onto the stack; this frame maps
this
and the formal parameters to the values for the receiver and other arguments, and the continuation to the body of the method.
Method bodies are expected to store their return values in the implicitly defined variable
res
10
10

10

For ease of presentation, we omit assignment to
res
in methods returning
void
.
. When the continuation is empty (
\epsilon
), the frame is popped and the value of
res

from the popped frame is stored in the variable map of the top frame.

Thus, when
\overline{M};\sigma\!\dashrightarrow\!{\sigma^{\prime}}
is within the same method we have
|\sigma^{\prime}|
=
|\sigma|
; when it is a call we have

|\sigma^{\prime}|
=
|\sigma|+1
; and when it is a return we have
|\sigma^{\prime}|
=
|\sigma|-1
.
Fig.
3
from §
2
distinguishes
{\dashrightarrow}
execution steps into: steps within the same call (
\rightarrow
), entering a method (
\uparrow
), returning from a method (
\downarrow
).
Therefore
\overline{M};\sigma_{8}\!\dashrightarrow\!{\sigma_{9}}
is a step within the same call,

\overline{M};\sigma_{9}\!\dashrightarrow\!{\sigma_{10}}
is a method entry with
\overline{M};\sigma_{12}\!\dashrightarrow\!{\sigma_{13}}

the corresponding return.
In general,
\overline{M};\sigma\!\dashrightarrow^{*}\!{\sigma^{\prime}}
may involve any number of calls or returns:
e.g.

\overline{M};\sigma_{10}\!\dashrightarrow^{*}\!{\sigma_{15}}
, involves no calls and two returns.

3.3.
Fundamental Concepts

The novel features of our assertions — protection and scoped invariants
— are both defined in terms of the current point of execution.
Therefore, for the semantics of our assertions we need to represent calls and returns, scoped execution, and (in)directly accessible objects.

3.3.1.
Method Calls and Returns

These are characterized through pushing/popping frames :

\ {\sigma}\,{\triangledown}\,{\phi}
pushes
frame
\phi
onto the stack of
\sigma
, while

{\sigma\,\,{\vartriangle}\,}
pops the top frame and updates the continuation and variable map.

Definition 3.1.

Given a state
\sigma
, and a frame
\phi
, we define

•

\ {\sigma}\,{\triangledown}\,{\phi}\ \triangleq\ ({\overline{\phi}\cdot\phi},\chi)
if
\sigma=(\overline{\phi},\chi)
.

•

\ \sigma\,\,{\vartriangle}\,\ \ \ \triangleq\ {(\overline{\phi}\cdot(\phi_{n}[\mbox{{\texttt{cont}}}\mapsto stmt][x\mapsto\lfloor\mbox{{\texttt{res}}}\rfloor_{\phi_{n}}]),\chi)}
if

\hskip 42.67912pt\sigma=(\overline{\phi}\cdot\phi_{n}\cdot\phi_{n+1},\chi)
, and
\phi_{n}(\mbox{{\texttt{cont}}})\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny txt}}}}{{=}}}x:=y_{0}.m(\overline{y});stmt

Consider Fig.
3
again:
\sigma_{8}=\ {\sigma_{7}}\,{\triangledown}\,{\phi}
for some
\phi
, and
\sigma_{15}
=
\sigma_{14}\,{\vartriangle}\,
.

3.3.2.
Scoped Execution

In order to give semantics to scoped invariants (introduced in §
2.1.2
and to be fully defined in Def.
5.4
), we need a new definition of execution, called
scoped execution
.

Definition 3.2 (Scoped Execution).

Given modules
\overline{M}
, and states
\sigma
,
\sigma_{1}
,
\sigma_{n}
, and
\sigma^{\prime}
, we define:

•

\overline{M};\,{\sigma}\rightsquigarrow\sigma^{\prime}\ \ \ \,\ \ \ \triangleq\ \ \ \overline{M};\sigma\!\dashrightarrow\!{\sigma^{\prime}}\,\wedge\,|\sigma|\leq|\sigma^{\prime}|

•

\overline{M};\,{\sigma_{1}}{\rightsquigarrow^{*}}\sigma_{n}\ \ \,\ \ \ \triangleq\ \ \ {\sigma_{1}}={\sigma_{n}}\ \ \vee\ \ \exists\sigma_{2},...\sigma_{n-1}.\forall i\!\in\![1..n)[\ \overline{M};\sigma_{i}\!\dashrightarrow\!{\sigma_{i+1}}\ \wedge\ |\sigma_{1}|\leq|\sigma_{i+1}|\ ]

•

\overline{M};\,{\sigma}{\rightsquigarrow^{*}}\!\!_{\mathit{fin}}\,\sigma^{\prime}\ \,\ \ \ \triangleq\ \ \ \overline{M};\,{\sigma}{\rightsquigarrow^{*}}\sigma^{\prime}\ \wedge\ \ {|\sigma|=|\sigma^{\prime}|\ \ \wedge\ \ \sigma^{\prime}.\mbox{{\texttt{cont}}}=\epsilon}

Consider Fig.
3
:
Here
|\sigma_{8}|\leq|\sigma_{9}|

and thus
\overline{M};\,{\sigma_{8}}\rightsquigarrow\sigma_{9}
.
Also,
\overline{M};\sigma_{14}\!\dashrightarrow\!{\sigma_{15}}
but
|\sigma_{14}|\not\leq|\sigma_{15}|

(this step returns from the active call in
\sigma_{14}
),
and hence
\overline{M};\,{\sigma_{14}}\not\rightsquigarrow\sigma_{15}
.
Finally, even though
|\sigma_{8}|=|\sigma_{18}|

and
\overline{M};\sigma_{8}\!\dashrightarrow^{*}\!{\sigma_{18}}
, we have

\overline{M};\,{\sigma_{8}}\not\!\!\!{\rightsquigarrow^{*}}\sigma_{18}
:
This is so, because the execution
\overline{M};\sigma_{8}\!\dashrightarrow^{*}\!{\sigma_{18}}
goes through the step

\overline{M};\sigma_{14}\!\dashrightarrow\!{\sigma_{15}}
and
|\sigma_{8}|\not\leq|\sigma_{15}|

(this step returns from the active call in
\sigma_{8}
).

The relation
{\rightsquigarrow^{*}}
contains more than the transitive closure of
\rightsquigarrow
.

E.g.,
,
{\overline{M};\,{\sigma_{9}}{\rightsquigarrow^{*}}\sigma_{13}}
, even though
{\overline{M};\,{\sigma_{9}}\rightsquigarrow\sigma_{12}}
and
{\overline{M};\,{\sigma_{12}}\not\!\!\!{\rightsquigarrow^{*}}\sigma_{13}}
.
Lemma
3.3
says that the value of the parameters does not change during execution of the same method.
Appendix
B
discusses proofs, and further properties.

Lemma 3.3.

For all
\overline{M}
,
\sigma
,
\sigma^{\prime}
:

\hskip 8.5359pt\overline{M};\,{\sigma}{\rightsquigarrow^{*}}\sigma^{\prime}\ \wedge\ |\sigma|=|\sigma^{\prime}|\ \ \ \Longrightarrow\ \ \ \forall x\in Prms(\overline{M},\sigma).[\ \lfloor x\rfloor_{\sigma}=\lfloor x\rfloor_{\sigma^{\prime}}\ ]

3.3.3.
Reachable Objects, Locally Reachable Objects, and Well-formed States

To define protection (no external object indirectly accessible from the top frame
has access to the protected object,
c.f.
§
2.1.1
) we first define
reachability. An object

\alpha
is

locally reachable
, i.e.
\alpha\in LocRchbl(\sigma)
, if it is reachable from the top frame on the stack of
\sigma
.

Definition 3.4.

We define

•

Rchbl(\alpha,\sigma)\ \ \ \ \ \triangleq\ \ \ \{\ \alpha^{\prime}\,\mid\ \exists n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}.\exists f_{1},...f_{n}.[\ \lfloor\alpha.f_{1}...f_{n}\rfloor_{\sigma}=\alpha^{\prime}\ ]\ \}
.

•

LocRchbl(\sigma)\ \ \ \triangleq\ \ \ \{\ \alpha\ \mid\ {\exists x\in dom(\sigma)\wedge\alpha\in Rchbl(\lfloor x\rfloor_{\sigma},\sigma)\ \}}
.

In well-formed states,
\overline{M}\models\sigma
, the value of a parameter in any callee (
{\sigma[k]}
) is also the value of some variable in the caller (
{\sigma[k\!-\!1]}
),
and any address reachable from any frame (
{LocRchbl(\sigma[k])}
) is reachable from some formal parameter of that frame.

Definition 3.5 (Well-formed states).

For modules
\overline{M}
, and states
\sigma
,
\sigma^{\prime}
:

\overline{M}\models\sigma\ \ \triangleq\ \ \forall k\in\mathbb{N}.[\ 1<k\leq|\sigma|\ \Longrightarrow

\hskip 64.01869pt[\ \ \forall x\in Prms(\sigma[k],\overline{M}).[\ \exists y.\ \lfloor x\rfloor_{\sigma[k]}=\lfloor y\rfloor_{\sigma[k\!-\!1]}\ ]

\hskip 8.5359pt\ \ \ \wedge

\hskip 71.13188pt{LocRchbl(\sigma[k])}=\bigcup_{z\in{Prms(\sigma[k],\overline{M})}}{Rchbl(\lfloor z\rfloor_{\sigma[k]},\sigma)}\ \ \ \ \ \ ]

Lemma
3.6
says that
(
1
) execution preserves well-formedness, and
(
2
) any object which is locally reachable after pushing a frame was locally reachable before pushing that frame.

Lemma 3.6.

For all modules
\overline{M}
, states
\sigma
,
\sigma^{\prime}
, and frame
\phi
:

(1)

\overline{M}\models\sigma\ \wedge\ {\overline{M},\sigma\dashrightarrow\ \sigma^{\prime}}\ \ \Longrightarrow\ \ \overline{M}\models\sigma^{\prime}

(2)

\sigma^{\prime}=\ {\sigma}\,{\triangledown}\,{\phi}\ \wedge\ \overline{M}\models\sigma^{\prime}\ \ \Longrightarrow\ \ LocRchbl(\sigma^{\prime})\ \subseteq LocRchbl(\sigma)

4.
Assertions

Our assertions are standard (
e.g.
properties of the values of expressions, connectives, quantification
etc.
) or about protection (
i.e.

{\langle\!\!\langle{\mathit{e}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!{\mathit{e}}}
and

{\langle\!\!\langle{\mathit{e}}\rangle\!\!\rangle}
).

Definition 4.1.

Assertions,
A
, are defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lllr}A&::=&{\mathit{e}}\;\;|\;\;{\mathit{e}}:C\;\;|\;\;\neg A\;\;|\;\;A\ \wedge\ A\;\;|\;\;\forall x:C.A\;\;|\;\;{\mathit{e}}:\mbox{{\texttt{extl}}}\;\;|\;\;\langle\!\!\langle{\mathit{e}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!{\mathit{e}}\;\;|\;\;\langle\!\!\langle{\mathit{e}}\rangle\!\!\rangle&\text{\bf{}}\\
\\
\end{array}

11
11

11

Addresses in assertions as
e.g.
in
\alpha.blnce>700
, are useful when giving semantics to universal quantifiers

c.f.
Def.
4.3
.(
5
), when the local map changes
e.g.
upon call and return, and in general, for scoped invariants,
c.f.
Def.
5.4
.

{\hskip-1.0ptF\hskip-1.0pt}v(A)
returns the free variables in
A
; for example,
{\hskip-1.0ptF\hskip-1.0pt}v(a\!:\!Account\wedge\forall b:int.[a.=b])
=
\{a\}
.

Definition 4.2 (Shorthands).

We write
\mathit{e}:\mbox{{\texttt{intl}}}
for
\neg(\mathit{e}:\mbox{{\texttt{extl}}})
, and

extl
. resp.
intl
for
\mbox{{\texttt{this}}}:\mbox{{\texttt{extl}}}
resp.
\mbox{{\texttt{this}}}:\mbox{{\texttt{intl}}}
. Forms such as
A\rightarrow A^{\prime}
,
A\vee A^{\prime}
, and
\exists x:C.A
can be encoded.

Satisfaction of Assertions by a module and a state is expressed through
M,\sigma\models{A}
and defined by cases on the shape of
A
, in definitions
4.3
and

4.4
.

M
is used to look up the definitions of ghost fields, and to find class definitions to determine whether an object is external.

4.1.
Semantics of assertions – first part

To determine satisfaction of an expression, we use the evaluation relation,
M,\sigma,e\hookrightarrow v
,
which says that the expression
e
evaluates
to value
v
in the context of state
\sigma
and module
M
.
Ghost fields may be recursively defined, thus evaluation of
\mathit{e}
might
not terminate. Nevertheless, the logic of assertions remains classical because recursion is restricted to expressions.

Definition 4.3 (Satisfaction
of Assertions – first part).

We define satisfaction of an assertion
A
by a state
\sigma
with
module
M
as:

(1)

M,\sigma\models{\mathit{e}}\ \ \ \triangleq\ \ \ M,\sigma,{\mathit{e}}\hookrightarrow\texttt{true}

(2)

M,\sigma\models{{\mathit{e}}}:C\ \ \ \triangleq\ \ \ M,\sigma,{\mathit{e}}\hookrightarrow\alpha\ \wedge\ \textit{classOf}(\alpha,\sigma)=C

(3)

M,\sigma\models\neg A\ \ \ \triangleq\ \ \ {M},{\sigma}\not\models{A}

(4)

M,\sigma\models A_{1}\ \wedge\ A_{2}\ \ \ \triangleq\ \ \ M,\sigma\models A_{1}\ \wedge\ M,\sigma\models A_{2}

(5)

M,\sigma\models\forall x:C.A\ \ \ \triangleq\ \ \ \forall\alpha.[\ M,\sigma\models\alpha:C\ \Longrightarrow\ M,\sigma\models A[\alpha/x]\ ]

(6)

M,\sigma\models{\mathit{e}}:\mbox{{\texttt{extl}}}\ \ \ \triangleq\ \ \ \exists C.[\ M,\sigma\models{{\mathit{e}}}:C\ \wedge\ C\notin M\ ]

Note that while execution takes place in the context of one or more modules,
\overline{M}
, satisfaction of assertions considers
exactly one
module
M
– the internal module.

M
is used to look up the definitions of ghost fields, and to determine whether objects are external.

4.2.
Semantics of Assertions - second part

In §
2.1.1
we introduced protection – we will now formalize this concept.

An object is protected from another object,
\langle\!\!\langle{\alpha}\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!{\alpha_{o}}
, if
the two objects are not equal, and no external object reachable from
a_{o}
has a field pointing to
\alpha
.
This ensures that the last element on any path leading from
\alpha_{o}
to
\alpha

in an internal object.

An object is protected,
\langle\!\!\langle{\alpha}\rangle\!\!\rangle
, if no external object
reachable from any of the current frame’s arguments has a field
pointing to
\alpha
; and furthermore, if the receiver is external,
then
no parameter to the current method call directly refers to
\alpha
.
This ensures that no external object reachable from the current
receiver or arguments can “obtain”
\alpha
, where obtain
\alpha
is either direct access through a field,
or by virtue of the method’s receiver being able to see all the arguments.

Definition 4.4 (Satisfaction
of Assertions – Protection).

– continuing definitions in
4.3
:

(1)

M,\sigma\models\langle\!\!\langle{\alpha}\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!{\alpha_{o}}\ \ \ \triangleq

(a)

\alpha\neq\alpha_{0}
,

(b)

\forall\alpha^{\prime}.\forall f.[\ \alpha^{\prime}\in{Rchbl(\alpha_{o},\sigma)}\wedge\ M,\sigma\models\alpha^{\prime}:\mbox{{\texttt{extl}}}\ \ \Longrightarrow\ \ \lfloor\alpha^{\prime}.f\rfloor_{\sigma}\neq\alpha\ ]
.

(2)

M,\sigma\models\langle\!\!\langle\alpha\rangle\!\!\rangle\ \ \ \triangleq\ \ \

(a)

M,\sigma\models\mbox{{\texttt{extl}}}\ \ \Longrightarrow\ \ \forall x\!\in\!\sigma.\ M,\sigma\models x\neq\alpha
,

(b)

\forall\alpha^{\prime}.\forall f.[\ \alpha^{\prime}\in{LocRchbl(\sigma)}\wedge\ M,\sigma\models\alpha^{\prime}:\mbox{{\texttt{extl}}}\ \ \Longrightarrow\ \ \lfloor\alpha^{\prime}.f\rfloor_{\sigma}\neq\alpha\ ]
.

Moreover,

\ \ \ \ \ \ (3)\ \ M,\sigma\models\langle\!\!\langle{\mathit{e}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!{\mathit{e}_{o}}

\triangleq

\exists\alpha,\alpha_{o}.[\ M,\sigma,{\mathit{e}}\hookrightarrow\alpha\ \wedge\ M,\sigma,{\mathit{e}_{0}}\hookrightarrow\alpha_{0}\ \wedge\ M,\sigma\models\langle\!\!\langle{\alpha}\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!{\alpha_{o}}\ ]
,

\ \ \ \ \ \ (4)\ \ M,\sigma\models\langle\!\!\langle\mathit{e}\rangle\!\!\rangle

\triangleq

\exists\alpha.[\ M,\sigma,{\mathit{e}}\hookrightarrow\alpha\ \wedge\ M,\sigma\models\langle\!\!\langle\alpha\rangle\!\!\rangle\ ]
.

We illustrate “protected” and “protected from” in Fig.
2
in §
2
.
and Fig.
13
in App.
C
.
In general,
\langle\!\!\langle{\alpha}\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!{\alpha_{o}}
ensures that
\alpha_{o}
will get access to
\alpha
only if another object
grants that access.
Similarly,
\langle\!\!\langle\alpha\rangle\!\!\rangle
ensures that during execution of the current method, no external object will get direct access to
\alpha
unless some internal object grants that access
12
12

12

This is in line with the motto ”only connectivity begets connectivity” from
(
Miller 2006
)
.
.
Thus, protection together with protection preservation (
i.e.
no internal object gives access) guarantee
lack of eventual external access.

Discussion

Lack of eventual
direct access is a central concept in the verification of code with calls to and callbacks from untrusted code.
It has already been over-approximated in several different ways,
e.g.
2nd-class
(
Osvald et al. 2016
;
Xhebraj et al. 2022
)

or borrowed (“2nd-hand”) references

(
Chan et al. 1998
;
Boyland 2001
)
,
textual modules
(
Mackay et al. 2022
)
,
information flow
(
Swasey et al. 2017
)
, runtime
checks
(
Andrici et al. 2024
)
,
abstract data type exports
(
Liu et al. 2023
)
,
separation-based invariants
Iris
(
Rao et al. 2023b
;
Georges et al. 2024
)
,
– more in §
8
.
In general, protection is applicable in more situations (i.e. is less
restrictive) than most of these approaches,
although more restrictive than the ideal “lack of eventual access”.

An alternative definition might consider
\alpha
as protected from
\alpha_{o}
,
if any path from
\alpha_{o}
to
\alpha
goes through at least one internal object.
With this definition,
o_{4}
would be protected from
o_{1}
in the heap shown here.
However,
o_{1}
can make a call to
o_{2}
, and this call could return
o_{3}
.
Once
o_{1}
has direct access to
o_{3}
, it can also get direct access to
o_{4}
.
The example justifies our current definition.

4.3.
Preservation of Assertions

Program logics require some form of framing,
i.e.
conditions under which satisfaction of assertions is preserved across program execution.
This is the subject of the current Section.

Def.
4.5
which turns an assertion
A
to the equivalent variable-free from by replacing all free variables from
A
by their values in
\sigma
. Then, Lemma
4.5
says that
satisfaction of an assertion is not affected by replacing free variables by their values, nor by changing the sate’s continuation.

Definition and Lemma 4.5.

For all
M
,
\sigma
,
stmt
,
A
, and
\overline{x}
where
\overline{x}={\hskip-1.0ptF\hskip-1.0pt}v{(A)}
:

•

{{}^{\,\sigma}\!\lceil A\rceil}\ \triangleq\ \ A[\overline{{\lfloor x\rfloor_{\sigma}}/x}]

•

M,\sigma\models A\ \ \ \Longleftrightarrow\ \ \ M,\sigma\models{{}^{\,\sigma}\!\lceil A\rceil}\ \ \ \Longleftrightarrow\ \ \ M,\sigma[\mbox{{\texttt{cont}}}\mapsto stmt]\models A

We now move to assertion preservation across method call and return.

4.3.1.
Stability

In most program logics, satisfaction of variable-free assertions is preserved when pushing/popping frames
–
i.e.
immediately after entering a method or returning from it.
But this is not so for our assertions, where protection depends on the heap but also
on the range of the top frame.
E.g.,
Fig.
2
:
\sigma_{2}\not\models\langle\!\!\langle o_{6}\rangle\!\!\rangle
, but after pushing a frame, we have
\sigma_{3}\models\langle\!\!\langle o_{6}\rangle\!\!\rangle
.

Assertions which do not contain
\langle\!\!\langle\_\rangle\!\!\rangle
are called
{Stbl(\_)}
,
while assertions which do not contain
\langle\!\!\langle\_\rangle\!\!\rangle
in
negative
positions are called
{Stb^{+}(\_)}
.
Fig.
6
shows some examples.
Lemma
4.6
says that
{Stbl(\_)}
assertions are preserved when pushing/popping frames,
and
{Stb^{+}(\_)}
assertions are preserved when pushing internal frames.

C.f.
Appendix
D
for definitions and proofs.

Lemma 4.6.

For all states
\sigma
, frames
\phi
, all assertions
A
with
{\hskip-1.0ptF\hskip-1.0pt}v(A)=\emptyset

•

{Stbl(A)}\ \ \ \Longrightarrow\ \ \ [\ \ M,\sigma\models A\ \ \Longleftrightarrow\ \ M,{\sigma\,{\triangledown}\,\phi}\models A\ \ ]

•

{Stb^{+}(A)}\ \wedge\ {M\cdot\overline{M}\models{\sigma\,{\triangledown}\,\phi}}\ \wedge\ M,{\sigma\,{\triangledown}\,\phi}\models\mbox{{\texttt{intl}}}\ \wedge\ M,\sigma\models A

\ \ \ \Longrightarrow\ \ \ M,{\sigma\,{\triangledown}\,\phi}\models A\

While
Stb^{+}
assertions
are
preserved when pushing internal frames, they are
not
necessarily preserved when pushing external frames
nor when popping frames (
c.f.
Ex.
4.7
).

Example 4.7.

Fig.
2
illustrates that

–
Stb^{+}
not necessarily preserved by External Push
:
Namely,
\sigma_{2}\models\langle\!\!\langle o_{4}\rangle\!\!\rangle
, pushing frame
\phi_{3}
with an external receiver
and
o_{4}
as argument gives
\sigma_{3}
, we have
\sigma_{3}\not\models\langle\!\!\langle o_{4}\rangle\!\!\rangle
.

–
Stb^{+}
not necessarily preserved by Pop
: Namely,
\sigma_{3}\models\langle\!\!\langle o_{6}\rangle\!\!\rangle
, returning from
\sigma_{3}
would give
\sigma_{2}
, and we have
\sigma_{2}\not\models\langle\!\!\langle o_{6}\rangle\!\!\rangle
.

We work with
Stb^{+}
assertions (the
Stbl
requirement is too strong).
But we need to address the lack of preservation of
Stb^{+}
assertions for external method calls and returns.
We do the former through
adaptation
(
{-\!\,\!{\triangledown}}{}
in Sect
6.2.2
), and the latter through

deep satisfaction
(§
7
).

4.3.2.
Encapsulation

As external code is unknown, it could, in principle, have unlimited effect and invalidate any assertion, and thus make reasoning about external calls impossible.
However, because fields are private, assertions which read internal fields only, cannot be invalidated by external execution steps.
Reasoning about external calls relies on such
encapsulated
assertions.

Judgment
M\ \vdash{Enc(A)}
from Def
D.4
, expresses
A
looks up the contents of
internal objects only, does not contain
\langle\!\!\langle\_\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!\_
, and does not contain
\langle\!\!\langle\_\rangle\!\!\rangle
in negative positions.
Lemma
4.8
says that
M\ \vdash{Enc(A)}
says
that any external scoped execution step which involves
M
and any set of other modules
\overline{M}
, preserves satisfaction of
A
.

\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{|c|c|c|c|c|c|}\hline\cr&\ \ \mbox{{\texttt{z.f}}}\geq 3&\ \ \langle\!\!\langle\mbox{{\texttt{x}}}\rangle\!\!\rangle&\ \ \neg(\ \langle\!\!\langle\mbox{{\texttt{x}}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\ )&\ \ \langle\!\!\langle\mbox{{\texttt{y}}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!\mbox{{\texttt{x}}}&\ \ \neg(\ \langle\!\!\langle\mbox{{\texttt{y}}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!\mbox{{\texttt{x}}}\ )\\
\hline\cr{Stbl(\_)}&\checkmark&\times&\times&\checkmark&\checkmark\\
\hline\cr{Stb^{+}(\_)}&\checkmark&\checkmark&\times&\checkmark&\checkmark\\
\hline\cr{Enc(\_)}&\checkmark&\checkmark&\times&\times&\times\\
\hline\cr\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 6
.
Comparing
{Stbl(\_)}
,
{Stb^{+}(\_)}
, and
{Enc(\_)}
assertions.

Lemma 4.8 (Encapsulation).

For all modules
M
, and assertions
A
:

•

M\ \vdash\ {Enc(A)}\ \Longrightarrow\ \ \forall\overline{M},\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}.[\ \ M,\sigma\models(A\wedge\mbox{{\texttt{extl}}})\ \wedge\ {M\!\cdot\!\overline{M};\,{\sigma}\rightsquigarrow\sigma^{\prime}}\ \Longrightarrow\ {M},{\sigma^{\prime}}\models{{}^{\,\sigma}\!\lceil A\rceil}\ \ ]

5.
Specifications

We now discuss syntax and semantics of our specifications, and illustrate them through examples.

5.1.
Syntax, Semantics, Examples

Definition 5.1 (Specifications Syntax).

We define the syntax of specifications,
S
:

\begin{array}[]{lllr}S&::=&\ \ \rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{{$\mathbb{A}\!\!$}}{\overline{x:C}}.{{\{}}{A}{{\}}}\ \;\;|\;\;\ \{\,A\,\}\,p\ C\!::\!m(\overline{y:C})\,\{\,A\,\}\,\,\parallel\,\,\{A\}\ \;\;|\;\;\ S\,\wedge\,S&\text{\bf{ }}\\
\vskip-12.0pt plus -4.0pt minus -4.0pt\ p&::=&\ \mbox{{\texttt{private}}}\ \;\;|\;\;\ \mbox{{\texttt{public}}}&\text{\bf{ }}\\
\end{array}

In Def.
5.6
later on we describe well-formedness of
S
, but we first discuss semantics and some examples.

We use quadruples involving states:
{\overline{M};M\,\models\,\{\,A\,\}\,\sigma\,\{\,A^{\prime}\,\}\,\parallel\,\{\,A^{\prime\prime}\,\}}

says that if
\sigma
satisfies
A
, then any terminating scoped execution of its continuation (
{\overline{M}\!\cdot\!M;\,{\sigma}{\rightsquigarrow^{*}}\!\!_{\mathit{fin}}\,\sigma^{\prime}}
) will satisfy
A^{\prime}
, and any intermediate reachable external state
(
{\overline{M}\!\cdot\!M;\,{\sigma}{\rightsquigarrow^{*}}\sigma^{\prime\prime}}
) will satisfy the “mid-condition”,
A^{\prime\prime}
.

Definition 5.2.

For modules
\overline{M}
,
M
, state
\sigma
, and assertions
A
,
A^{\prime}
and
A^{\prime\prime}
, we define:

•

{\overline{M};M\,\models\,\{\,A\,\}\,\sigma\,\{\,A^{\prime}\,\}\,\parallel\,\{\,A^{\prime\prime}\,\}}\ \ \triangleq\ \ \forall\sigma^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime\prime}.[

\hskip 5.69046ptM,\sigma\models{A}\ \ \Longrightarrow\ \ [\ \ {\overline{M}\!\cdot\!M;\,{\sigma}{\rightsquigarrow^{*}}\!\!_{\mathit{fin}}\,\sigma^{\prime}}\ \ \Longrightarrow\ \ M,\sigma^{\prime}\models{A^{\prime}}\ \ \ \ ]\ \ \ \wedge

\ \hskip 71.13188pt[\ \ {\overline{M}\!\cdot\!M;\,{\sigma}{\rightsquigarrow^{*}}\sigma^{\prime\prime}}\ \ \ \Longrightarrow\ \ M,\sigma^{\prime\prime}\models{(\mbox{{\texttt{extl}}}\rightarrow{{}^{\,\sigma}\!\lceil A^{\prime\prime}\rceil})}\ \ \ ]\ \ \ \ \ ]

Example 5.3.

Consider
{...;...\,\models\,\{\,A_{1}\,\}\,\sigma_{4}\,\{\,A_{2}\,\}\,\parallel\,\{\,A_{3}\,\}}

for Fig.
3
.
It means that if
\sigma_{4}
satisfies
A_{1}
,
then
\sigma_{23}
will satisfy
A_{2}
, while
\sigma_{6}
-
\sigma_{9}
,
\sigma_{13}
-
\sigma_{17}
, and
\sigma_{20}
-
\sigma_{21}
will satisfy
A_{3}
.
It does not imply anything about
\sigma_{24}
because
...;\,{\sigma_{4}}\not\!\!\!{\rightsquigarrow^{*}}\sigma_{24}
.
Similarly, if
\sigma_{8}
satisfies
A_{1}

then
\sigma_{14}
will satisfy
A_{2}
, and
\sigma_{8}
,
\sigma_{9}
,
\sigma_{13}
,
\sigma_{14}
will satisfy
A_{3}
, while making no claims about
\sigma_{10}
,
\sigma_{11}
,
\sigma_{12}
, nor about
\sigma_{15}
onwards.

Now we define
M\models\ \rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{{$\mathbb{A}\!\!$}}{\overline{x:C}}.{{\{}}{A}{{\}}}

to mean that if an external state
\sigma
satisfies
A
, then all future external states reachable from
\sigma
—including nested
calls and returns but
stopping
before returning from the active call in
\sigma
— also satisfy
A
.
And
M\models\{\,A_{1}\,\}\,p\ D\!::\!m(\overline{y:D})\,\{\,A_{2}\,\}\,\,\parallel\,\,\{A_{3}\}
means that scoped execution of a call to
m
from
D
in states satisfying
A_{1}
leads to final states satisfying
A_{2}
(if it terminates),
and to intermediate external states satisfying
A_{3}
.

Definition 5.4 (Semantics of Specifications).

We define
M\models{S}
by cases over
S
:

(1)

M\models\ \rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{{$\mathbb{A}\!\!$}}{\overline{x:C}}.{{\{}}{A}{{\}}}\ \ \ \triangleq\ \ \ {\forall\overline{M},\sigma.[\ {\overline{M};M\,\models\,\{\,\mbox{{\texttt{extl}}}\wedge\overline{x:C}\wedge A\,\}\,\sigma\,\{\,A\,\}\,\parallel\,\{\,A\,\}}\ ].}

(2)

M\models\{\,A_{1}\,\}\,p\ D\!::\!m(\overline{y:D})\,\{\,A_{2}\,\}\,\,\parallel\,\,\{A_{3}\}\ \ \ \ \triangleq

\ \ \forall\overline{M},\sigma,y_{0},\overline{y}.[\ \ \sigma.\mbox{{\texttt{cont}}}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny txt}}}}{{=}}}{u:=y_{0}.m(y_{1},..y_{n})}\ \ \Longrightarrow\ \

\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {\overline{M};M\,\models\,\{\,y_{0}\!:\!D,\overline{y\!:\!D}\wedge A[y_{0}/\mbox{{\texttt{this}}}]\,\}\,\ \sigma\ \,\{\,A_{2}[u/res,y_{0}/\mbox{{\texttt{this}}}]\,\}\,\parallel\,\{\,A_{3}\,\}}\ \ \ ]

(3)

M\models S\,\wedge\,S^{\prime}

\triangleq

M\models S\ \wedge\ M\models S^{\prime}

Fig.
3
in §
2.1.2
illustrated the meaning of
{\ \rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{{$\mathbb{A}\!\!$}}{\overline{x:C}}.{{\{}}{A_{0}}{{\}}}}
.
Moreover,
M_{good}\models S_{2}\wedge S_{3}\wedge S_{4}
, and
M_{fine}\models S_{2}\wedge S_{3}\wedge S_{4}
,
while
M_{bad}\not\models S_{2}
.
We continue with some examples – more in Appendix
E
.

Example 5.5 (Scoped Invariants and Method Specs).

S_{5}
says that non-null keys are immutable:

\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ S_{5}

\triangleq

{\ \rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{{$\mathbb{A}\!\!$}}{\mbox{{\texttt{a}}}:\mbox{{\texttt{Account}}},\mbox{{\texttt{k}}}:\mbox{{\texttt{Key}}}}.{{\{}}{\mbox{{\texttt{null}}}\neq\mbox{{\texttt{k}}}=\mbox{{\texttt{a.key }}}}{{\}}}}

S_{9}
guarantees that
set
preserves the protectedness of any account, and any key.

{\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ S_{9}}

\triangleq

\{\,a:\mbox{{\texttt{Account}}},a^{\prime}:\mbox{{\texttt{Account}}}\wedge\langle\!\!\langle a\rangle\!\!\rangle\wedge\langle\!\!\langle a^{\prime}.\mbox{{\texttt{key}}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\,\}\,

\ \ \ \ \ \ \mbox{{\texttt{public Account}}}::\mbox{{\texttt{set}}}({\mbox{{\texttt{key'}}}:\mbox{{\texttt{Key}}}})

\{\,\langle\!\!\langle a\rangle\!\!\rangle\wedge\langle\!\!\langle a^{\prime}.\mbox{{\texttt{key}}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\,\}\ {\,\parallel\,\ \{\langle\!\!\langle a\rangle\!\!\rangle\wedge\langle\!\!\langle a^{\prime}.\mbox{{\texttt{key}}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\}}

Note that
a
,
a^{\prime}
are disjoint from
this
and the formal parameters of
set
.
In that sense,
a
and
a^{\prime}
are universally quantified; a call of
set
will preserve protectedness for
all
accounts and their keys.

5.2.
Well-formedness

We now define what it means for a specification to be well-formed:

Definition 5.6.

Well-formedness
of specifications,
\vdash S
, is defined by cases on
S
:

•

\ \vdash{\ \rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{{$\mathbb{A}\!\!$}}{\overline{x:C}}.{{\{}}{A}{{\}}}}\ \ \ \triangleq\ \ \ {\hskip-1.0ptF\hskip-1.0pt}v(A)\subseteq\{\overline{x}\}\,\wedge\,{M\vdash{Enc({\overline{x:C}}\wedge A)}}
.

•

\ \vdash{\{\,\overline{x:C^{\prime}}\wedge A\,\}\,p\ C\!::\!m(\overline{y:C})\,\{\,A^{\prime}\,\}\,\,\parallel\,\,\{A^{\prime\prime}\}}\ \ \ \triangleq

\hskip 19.91684pt[\ \ {\small{{\mbox{{\texttt{res}}},\mbox{{\texttt{this}}}\!\notin\!\overline{x},\overline{y}}\ \wedge\ {{\hskip-1.0ptF\hskip-1.0pt}v(A)\!\subseteq\!\overline{x},\overline{y},\mbox{{\texttt{this}}}}\ \wedge\ {\hskip-1.0ptF\hskip-1.0pt}v(A^{\prime})\!\subseteq\!\overline{x},\overline{y},\mbox{{\texttt{this}}},\mbox{{\texttt{res}}}\ \wedge\ {\hskip-1.0ptF\hskip-1.0pt}v(A^{\prime\prime})\!\subseteq\!{\overline{x}}}}

\hskip 19.91684pt\ \ \ \wedge\ {Stb^{+}(A)}\,\ \wedge\ \,{Stb^{+}(A^{\prime})}\,\ \wedge\ \,M\vdash{Enc(\overline{x:C^{\prime}}\wedge A^{\prime\prime})}\ \ \ ]

•

\ \vdash S\,\wedge\,S^{\prime}\ \ \triangleq\ \ \vdash S\,\ \wedge\,\ \vdash S^{\prime}
.

Def
5.6
’s requirements about free variables are relatively straightforward – more in. §
E.1.1
.

Def
5.6
’s requirements about encapsulation are motivated by Def.
5.4
. If
\overline{x:C}\wedge A
in the scoped invariant were not encapsulated, then it could be invalidated by some external code, and it would be impossible to ever satisfy Def.
5.4
(
1
). Similarly, if a method specification’s mid-condition,
A^{\prime\prime}
, could be invalidated by some external code, then it would be impossible to ever satisfy Def.
5.4
(
2
).

Def
5.6
’s requirements about stability are motivated by our Hoare logic rule for internal calls,
[Call_Int]
, Fig
8
. The requirement
{Stb^{+}(A)}
for the method’s precondition gives that
A
is preserved when an internal frame is pushed,
c.f.
Lemma
4.6
.
The requirement
{Stb^{+}(A^{\prime})}
for the method’s postcondition gives, in the context of deep satisfaction, that
A^{\prime}
is preserved when an internal frame is popped,
c.f.
Lemma
G.42
. This is crucial for soundness of
[Call_Int]
.

5.3.
Discussion

Difference with Object and History Invariants.
Our scoped invariants are similar to, but different from, history invariants and object invariants.
We compare through an example:

Consider
\sigma_{a}
making a call transitioning to
\sigma_{b}
, execution of
\sigma_{b}
’s continuation eventually resulting in
\sigma_{c}
, and
\sigma_{c}
returning to
\sigma_{d}
.
Suppose all four states are external, and the module guarantees
\ \rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{{$\mathbb{A}\!\!$}}{\overline{x:Object}}.{{\{}}{A}{{\}}}
, and
\sigma_{a}\not\models A
, but
\sigma_{b}\models A
.
Scoped invariants ensure
\sigma_{c}\models A
, but allow
\sigma_{d}\not\models A
.

History invariants

(
Liskov and Wing 1994
;
Leino and Schulte 2007
;
Cohen et al. 2010
)
, instead, consider all future states including any method returns, and therefore would require that
\sigma_{d}\models A
. Thus, they are, for our purposes, both

unenforceable
and overly
restrictive
.
Unenforceable
: Take
A\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny txt}}}}{{=}}}\langle\!\!\langle\mbox{{\texttt{acc.key}}}\rangle\!\!\rangle
, assume in
\sigma_{a}
a path to an external object which has access to
acc.key
, assume that path is unknown in
\sigma_{b}
: then, the transition from
\sigma_{b}
to
\sigma_{c}
cannot eliminate that path—hence,
\sigma_{d}\not\models\langle\!\!\langle\mbox{{\texttt{acc.key}}}\rangle\!\!\rangle
.
Restrictive
: Take
A\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny txt}}}}{{=}}}\langle\!\!\langle\mbox{{\texttt{acc.key}}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\wedge a.\mbox{{\texttt{blnce}}}\geq b
; then, requiring
A
to hold in all states from
\sigma_{a}
until termination would prevent all future withdrawals from
a
, rendering the account useless.

Object invariants

(
Meyer 1992b
;
Meyer 1992a
;
Barnett et al. 2004
;
Leino and Müller 2004
;
Müller et al. 2006
)
, on the other hand, expect invariants to hold in all (visible) states,
here would require,
e.g.
that
\sigma_{a}\models A
. Thus, they are
inapplicable
for us: They would require,
e.g.
, that for all
acc
, in all (visible) states,
\langle\!\!\langle\mbox{{\texttt{acc.key}}}\rangle\!\!\rangle
, and thus prevent
any
withdrawals from
any
account in
any
state.

Difference between Postconditions and Invariants.

In all method specification examples so far, the post-condition and mid-condition were identical.
However, this need not be so.
Assume a method
tempLeak
defined in
Account
, with an external argument
extArg
, and method body:

extArg.m(this.key); this.key:=new Key

Then, the assertion
\langle\!\!\langle\mbox{{\texttt{this.key}}}\rangle\!\!\rangle
is invalidated by the external call
extArg.m(this.key)
, but is established by
this.key:=new Key
.
Therefore,
\langle\!\!\langle\mbox{{\texttt{this.key}}}\rangle\!\!\rangle
is a valid post-condition but not a valid mid-condition.
The specification of
tempLeak
could be

{\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ S_{\mbox{{\texttt{tempLeak}}}}}

\triangleq

\{\,\ \mbox{{\texttt{true}}}\ \,\}\,

\ \ \ \ \ \ \mbox{{\texttt{public Account}}}::\mbox{{\texttt{tempLeak}}}({\mbox{{\texttt{extArg}}}:\mbox{{\texttt{external}}}})

\{\,\ \langle\!\!\langle\mbox{{\texttt{this.key}}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\ \,\}\ {\,\parallel\,\ \{\ \mbox{{\texttt{true}}}\ \}}

Expressiveness

In §
E.2
we argue the expressiveness of our approach through a sequence of capability patterns studied in related approaches from the literature

(
Mackay et al. 2022
;
Devriese et al. 2016
;
Permenev et al. 2020
;
Rao et al. 2023a
;
Swasey et al. 2017
)
and written in our specification language.
These approaches are based on temporal logics
(
Permenev et al. 2020
;
Mackay et al. 2022
)
, or on extensions of Coq/Iris
(
Devriese et al. 2016
;
Rao et al. 2023a
;
Swasey et al. 2017
)
, and do not
offer Hoare logic rules for external calls.

6.
Hoare Logic

We develop an inference system for adherence to our specifications.
We distinguish three phases:

First Phase:
We assume an underlying Hoare logic,
{M\vdash_{ul}\{A\}{\ stmt\ }\{A^{\prime}\}}
, and extend it to a logic
{M\ \vdash\ \{\,A\,\}\ stmt\ \{\,A^{\prime}\,\}}
with the expected meaning,
i.e.
(*) execution of statement
stmt
in a state satisfying
A
will lead to a state satisfying
A^{\prime}
.
These triples only apply to
stmt
’s that do not contain method calls (even internal) – this is so, because method calls may make further calls to external methods.
In our extension we introduce judgements which
talk about protection.

Second Phase:
We develop a logic of quadruples
{M\ \vdash\ \{\,A\,\}\ stmt\ \{\,A^{\prime}\,\}\,\,\parallel\,\,\{\,A^{\prime\prime}\,\}}
. These promise (*) and
in addition, that (**) any intermediate external states reachable during execution of that
stmt

satisfy the
mid-condition

A^{\prime\prime}
.
We incorporate the triples from the first phase,
introduce mid-conditions, give the usual substructural rules, and deal with method calls.
For internal calls we use the methods’ specs. For external calls, we use the module’s invariants.

Third Phase:

We prove adherence to our specifications.
For method specifications we require that the body maps the precondition to the postcondition and preserves the method’s mid-condition.
For module invariants we require that they are preserved by all public methods of the module.

Preliminaries:
The judgement
{{\vdash\ \ M\ \ \mathbf{:}\ \ S}}
expresses that
S
is part of
M
’s specification.
In particular, it allows
safe renamings
.
These renamings are a convenience, akin to the Barendregt convention, and allow simpler Hoare rules –
c.f.
Sect.
6.3
,
Def.
F.1
, and Ex.
F.2
. We also require an underlying Hoare logic with judgements
M\vdash_{ul}\{A\}stmt\{A^{\prime}\}

–
c.f.
Ax.
F.3
.

6.1.
First Phase: Triples

In Fig.
7
we introduce our triples, of the form
{M\ \vdash\ \{\,A\,\}stmt\{\,A^{\prime}\,\}}
.
These promise, as expected, that any execution of
stmt
in a state satisfying
A
leads to a state satisfying
A^{\prime}
.

\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{lcl}\begin{array}[]{c}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {{\mbox{\hskip 14.22636pt [\sc{embed\_ul}]}}}\\
stmt\ \mbox{contains no method call}\\
{Stbl(A)}\ \ {Stbl(A^{\prime})}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {M\vdash_{ul}\{\ A\ \}{\ stmt\ }\{\ A^{\prime}\ \}}\\
\hline\cr M\ \vdash\ \{\,A\,\}\ stmt\ \{\,A^{\prime}\,\}\end{array}&&\begin{array}[]{c}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {{\mbox{\hskip 14.22636pt [\sc{prot-new}]}}}\\
{{u\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny txt}}}}{{\neq}}}x}}\\
\hline\cr M\ \vdash\ \{\,{{true}}\,\}\ u=\mbox{{\texttt{new}}}\ C\ \{\,\langle\!\!\langle u\rangle\!\!\rangle\ \wedge\langle\!\!\langle u\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!x\,\}\end{array}\\
\\
{\begin{array}[]{c}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {{\mbox{\hskip 14.22636pt [\sc{prot-1}]}}}\\
stmt\ \mbox{ is free of method cals, or assignment to $z$}\\
{M\ \vdash\ \{\,{A\wedge}\mathit{e}\!=\!z\,\}\ stmt\ \{\,\mathit{e}\!=\!z\,\}}\\
\hline\cr M\ \vdash\ \{\,\ {A\wedge}\langle\!\!\langle\mathit{e}\rangle\!\!\rangle\ \,\}\ stmt\ \{\,\ \langle\!\!\langle\mathit{e}\rangle\!\!\rangle\ \,\}\end{array}}&&{\begin{array}[]{c}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {{\mbox{\hskip 14.22636pt [\sc{prot-2}]}}}\\
stmt\mbox{ is either $x:=y$ or $x:=y.f$}\ \ \ \ \ z,z^{\prime}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny txt}}}}{{\neq}}}x\\
{M\ \vdash\ \{\,{A\wedge}z\!=\!\mathit{e}\wedge z^{\prime}\!\!=\!\!\mathit{e}^{\prime}\,\}\ stmt\{\,z\!=\!\mathit{e}\wedge z^{\prime}\!\!=\!\!\mathit{e}^{\prime}\,\}}\\
\hline\cr M\ \vdash\ \{\,\ {A\wedge}\langle\!\!\langle\mathit{e}\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!\mathit{e}^{\prime}\ \,\}\ stmt\{\,\ \langle\!\!\langle\mathit{e}\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!\mathit{e}^{\prime}\ \,\}\end{array}}\\
\\
{\begin{array}[]{c}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {{\mbox{\hskip 14.22636pt [\sc{prot-3}]}}}\\
{{x\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny txt}}}}{{\neq}}}z}}\\
\hline\cr M\ \vdash\ \{\,\ \langle\!\!\langle y.f\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!z\ \,\}\ x=y.f\ \{\,\ \langle\!\!\langle x\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!z\ \,\}\end{array}}&&{\begin{array}[]{c}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {{\mbox{\hskip 14.22636pt [\sc{prot-4}]}}}\\
\\
\hline\cr M\ \vdash\ \{\,\ \langle\!\!\langle x\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!z\ \wedge\ \langle\!\!\langle x\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!y^{\prime}\,\}\ y.f=y^{\prime}\ \{\,\ \langle\!\!\langle x\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!z\ \,\}\end{array}}\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 7.
Embedding the Underlying Hoare Logic, and Protection

With rule
embed_ul
in Fig.
7
, any triple
\{A\}stmt\{A^{\prime}\}
whose statement does not contain a method call, and which
can be proven in the underlying Hoare logic, can also be proven in our logic. In
Prot-1
, we see that protection of an object
o
is preserved by internal code which does not call any methods: namely any heap modifications will
ony affect internal objects, and this will not expose new, unmitigated external access to
o
.

Prot-2
,
Prot-3
and
Prot-4
describe the preservation of relative protection.
Proofs of soundness for these rules can be found in App.
G.5.1
.

6.2.
Second Phase: Quadruples

6.2.1.
Introducing mid-conditions, and substructural rules

We now introduce quadruple rules. Rule
mid
embeds triples
{M\ \vdash\ \{\,A\,\}\ s\ \{\,A^{\prime}\,\}}
into quadruples
{M\ \vdash\ \{\,A\,\}\ s\ \{\,A^{\prime}\,\}\,\,\parallel\,\,\{\,A^{\prime\prime}\,\}}
.
This is sound, because
stmt
is guaranteed not to contain method calls (by lemma
F.5
).

\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{c}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {{\mbox{[\sc{Mid}]}}}\\
M\ \vdash\ \{\,A\,\}\ stmt\ \{\,A^{\prime}\,\}\\
\hline\cr M\ \vdash\ \{\,A\,\}\ stmt\ \{\,A^{\prime}\,\}\,\,\parallel\,\,\{\,A^{\prime\prime}\,\}\end{array}\end{array}

Substructural quadruple rules appear in Fig.
16
, and are as expected:
Rules
sequ
and
consequ
are the usual rules for statement sequences and consequence, adapted to quadruples.
Rule
combine
combines two quadruples for the same statement into one.
Rule
Absurd
allows us to deduce anything out of
false
precondition, and
Cases
allows for case analysis.
These rules apply to
any
statements – even those containing method calls.

6.2.2.
Adaptation

In the outline of the Hoare proof of the external call in §
2.2
, we saw that an assertion of the form
\langle\!\!\langle x\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!\overline{y}
at the call site may imply
\langle\!\!\langle x\rangle\!\!\rangle
at entry to the call.
More generally, the
{-\!\,\!{\triangledown}}{}
operator adapts an assertion from the view of the callee to that of the caller,
and is used in the Hoare logic for method calls. It is defined below.

Definition 6.1.

[The
{-\!\,\!{\triangledown}}{}
operator]

\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{array}[]{rclcrcl}{\langle\!\!\langle\mathit{e}\rangle\!\!\rangle}\!-\!\!\!\,\!{\triangledown}\overline{y}&\triangleq&\langle\!\!\langle\mathit{e}\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!\overline{y}&&{(A_{1}\wedge A_{2})}\!-\!\!\!\,\!{\triangledown}\overline{y}&\triangleq&({A_{1}}\!-\!\!\!\,\!{\triangledown}\overline{y})\wedge({A_{2}}\!-\!\!\!\,\!{\triangledown}\overline{y})\\
{(\langle\!\!\langle\mathit{e}\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!\overline{u})}\!-\!\!\!\,\!{\triangledown}\overline{y}&\triangleq&\langle\!\!\langle\mathit{e}\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!\overline{u}&&{(\forall x:C.A)}\!-\!\!\!\,\!{\triangledown}\overline{y}&\triangleq&\forall x:C.({{A}\!-\!\!\!\,\!{\triangledown}\overline{y}})\\
{(\mathit{e}:\mbox{{\texttt{extl}}})}\!-\!\!\!\,\!{\triangledown}\overline{y}&\triangleq&{\mathit{e}:\mbox{{\texttt{extl}}}}&&{(\neg A)}\!-\!\!\!\,\!{\triangledown}\overline{y}&\triangleq&\neg({{A}\!-\!\!\!\,\!{\triangledown}\overline{y}})\\
{\mathit{e}}\!-\!\!\!\,\!{\triangledown}\overline{y}&\triangleq&\mathit{e}&&{(\mathit{e}:C)}\!-\!\!\!\,\!{\triangledown}\overline{y}&\triangleq&\mathit{e}:C\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}

Only the first equation in Def.
6.1
is interesting: for
\mathit{e}
to be protected at a callee with arguments
\overline{y}
, it should be protected from these arguments – thus

{\langle\!\!\langle\mathit{e}\rangle\!\!\rangle}\!-\!\!\!\,\!{\triangledown}\overline{y}=\langle\!\!\langle\mathit{e}\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!\overline{y}
.
The notation
\langle\!\!\langle\mathit{e}\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!\overline{y}
stands for
\langle\!\!\langle\mathit{e}\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!y_{0}\ \wedge\ ...\wedge\langle\!\!\langle\mathit{e}\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!y_{n}
, assuming that
\overline{y}
=
{y_{0},...y_{n}}
.

Lemma
6.2
states that
indeed,
{-\!\,\!{\triangledown}}{}
adapts assertions from the callee to the caller, and is the counterpart to the

\,{\triangledown}\,
.
In particular: (
1
):
{-\!\,\!{\triangledown}}{}
turns an assertion into a stable assertion.
(
2
): If the caller,
\sigma
, satisfies
A\,{\triangledown}\,Rng(\phi)
, then the callee,
\sigma\,{\triangledown}\,\phi
, satisfies
A
.
(
3
): When returning from external states, an assertion implies its adapted version.
(
4
): When calling from external states, an assertion implies its adapted version.

Lemma 6.2.

For states
\sigma
, assertions
A
, so that
Stb^{+}(A)
and
{\hskip-1.0ptF\hskip-1.0pt}v(A)=\emptyset
, frame
\phi
, variables
y_{0}
,
\overline{y}
:

(1)

{Stbl(\,A\!-\!\!\!\,\!{\triangledown}(y_{0},\overline{y})\,)}

(2)

M,\sigma\models A\!-\!\!\!\,\!{\triangledown}Rng(\phi)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \Longrightarrow\ \ \ \ M,\sigma\,{\triangledown}\,\phi\models A

(3)

M,\sigma\,{\triangledown}\,\phi\models A\wedge\mbox{{\texttt{extl}}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \Longrightarrow\ \ \ \ M,\sigma\models A\!-\!\!\!\,\!{\triangledown}Rng(\phi)

(4)

M,\sigma\models A\wedge\mbox{{\texttt{extl}}}\ \wedge\ M\cdot\overline{M}\models\sigma\,{\triangledown}\,\phi\ \ \ \ \ \ \Longrightarrow\ \ \ \ M,\sigma\,{\triangledown}\,\phi\models A\!-\!\!\!\,\!{\triangledown}Rng(\phi)

Proofs in Appendix
F.5
. Example
6.3

demonstrates the need for the
extl
requirement in (
3
).

Example 6.3 (When returning from internal states,
A
does not imply
A\!-\!\!\!\,\!{\triangledown}Rng(\phi)
).

In Fig.
2
we have

\sigma_{2}=\sigma_{1}\,{\triangledown}\,\phi_{2}
, and
\sigma_{2}\models\langle\!\!\langle o_{1}\rangle\!\!\rangle
, and
o_{1}\!\in\!Rng(\phi_{2})
,
but
\sigma_{1}\not\models\langle\!\!\langle o_{1}\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!o_{1}
.

6.2.3.
Reasoning about calls

is described in Fig.
8
.
Call_Int
for internal methods, whether public or private; and
Call_Ext_Adapt
and
Call_Ext_Adapt_Strong
for external methods.

\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{c}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {{\mbox{\hskip 133.72786pt [\sc{Call\_Int}]}}}\\
\begin{array}[]{c}{\vdash\ \ M\ \ \mathbf{:}\ \ \{\,A_{1}\,\}\,p\ C\!::\!m(\overline{x:C})\,\{\,A_{2}\,\}\,\,\parallel\,\,\{A_{3}\}}\\
{A_{1}^{\prime}=A_{1}[y_{0},\overline{y}/\mbox{{\texttt{this}}},\overline{x}]\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ A_{2}^{\prime}=A_{2}[y_{0},\overline{y},u/\mbox{{\texttt{this}}},\overline{x},\mbox{{\texttt{res}}}]}\end{array}\\
\hline\cr M\ \vdash\ \{\,\ y_{0}:C,\overline{y:C}\wedge{A_{1}^{\prime}}\ \,\}\ u:=y_{0}.m(y_{1},..y_{n})\ \{\,\ {A_{2}^{\prime}}\ \,\}\,\,\parallel\,\,\{\,\ A_{3}\ \,\}\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {{\mbox{\hskip 133.72786pt [\sc{Call\_Ext}\_Adapt]}}}\\
{\vdash\ \ M\ \ \mathbf{:}\ \ \ \rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{{$\mathbb{A}\!\!$}}{\overline{x:C}}.{{\{}}{A}{{\}}}}\\
\hline\cr M\ \vdash\ \{\,\ {y_{0}:\mbox{{\texttt{extl}}}}\,\wedge\,\overline{x:C}\ \wedge\ {A\!-\!\!\!\,\!{\triangledown}{(y_{0},\overline{y})}}\ \,\}\ u:=y_{0}.m(y_{1},..y_{n})\ \{\,\ {A\!-\!\!\!\,\!{\triangledown}{(y_{0},\overline{y})}}\ \,\}\,\,\parallel\,\,\{\,\ A\ \,\}\end{array}\\
\\
{\begin{array}[]{c}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {{\mbox{\hskip 133.72786pt [\sc{Call\_Ext}\_Adapt\_Strong]}}}\\
{\vdash\ \ M\ \ \mathbf{:}\ \ \ \rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{{$\mathbb{A}\!\!$}}{\overline{x:C}}.{{\{}}{A}{{\}}}}\\
\hline\cr M\ \vdash\ \{\,\ {y_{0}:\mbox{{\texttt{extl}}}}\,\wedge\,\overline{x:C}\ \wedge A\wedge\ {A\!-\!\!\!\,\!{\triangledown}{(y_{0},\overline{y})}}\ \,\}\ u:=y_{0}.m(y_{1},..y_{n})\ \{\,\ A\wedge{A\!-\!\!\!\,\!{\triangledown}{(y_{0},\overline{y})}}\ \,\}\,\,\parallel\,\,\{\,\ A\ \,\}\end{array}}\par\end{array}

Figure 8
.
Hoare Quadruples for Internal and External Calls – here
\overline{y}
stands for
y_{1},...y_{n}

For internal calls, we start, as usual, by looking up the method’s specification, and
substituting the formal by the actual parameters parameters (
\mbox{{\texttt{this}}},\overline{x}
by
y_{0},\overline{y}
).
Call_Int
is as expected: we require the precondition, and guarantee the postcondition and mid-condition.

Call_Int
is applicable whether the method is public or private.

For external calls, we consider the module’s invariants.
If the module promises to preserve
A
,
i.e.
if
{\vdash\ \ M\ \ \mathbf{:}\ \ \ \rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{{$\mathbb{A}\!\!$}}{\overline{x:D}}.{{\{}}{A}{{\}}}}
, and if its adapted version,
A\!-\!\!\!\,\!{\triangledown}(y_{0},\overline{y})
, holds before the call, then it also holds after the call (
Call_Ext_Adapt
).
If, in addition, the un-adapted version also holds before the call, then it also holds after the call (
Call_Ext_Adapt_Strong
).

Notice that internal calls,
Call_Int
, require the
un-adapted
method precondition (
i.e.

A_{1}^{\prime}
), while external calls, both
Call_Ext_Adapt
and
Call_Ext_Adapt_Strong
, require the

adapted
invariant (
i.e.

A\!-\!\!\!\,\!{\triangledown}(y_{0},\overline{y})
).
This is sound, because internal callees preserve
{Stb^{+}(\_)}
-assertions –
c.f.
Lemma
4.6
.
On the other hand, external callees do not necessarily preserve
{Stb^{+}(\_)}
-assertions –
c.f.
Ex.
4.7
.
Therefore, in order to guarantee that
A
holds upon entry to the callee, we need to know that
A\!-\!\!\!\,\!{\triangledown}(y_{0},\overline{y})
held at the caller site –
c.f.
Lemma
6.2
.

Remember that popping frames does not necessarily preserve

{Stb^{+}(\_)}
assertions –
c.f.
Ex.
4.7
.
Nevertheless,
Call_Int
guarantees the unadapted version,
A
, upon return from the call.
This is sound, because of our
deep satisfaction
of assertions – more in Sect.
7
.

Polymorphic Calls

Our rules do not
directly
address a scenario where the receiver may be
internal or external, and where the choice about this is made at runtime.
However, such scenaria are
indirectly
supported, through our rules of consequence and case-split.
More in Appendix
H.6
.

Example 6.4 (Proving external calls).

We continue our discussion from §
2.2
on how to establish the Hoare triple
(1)
:

(1?)

\{\ \ \mbox{{\texttt{buyer}}}:\mbox{{\texttt{extl}}}\ \wedge\ {\langle\!\!\langle\mbox{{\texttt{this.\mbox{{\texttt{accnt}}}.key}}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!\mbox{{\texttt{buyer}}}}\ \wedge\ \mbox{{\texttt{this.\mbox{{\texttt{accnt}}}.}}}=b\ \ \}

buyer.pay(this.accnt,price)

\{\ \ \ {\mbox{{\texttt{this.\mbox{{\texttt{accnt}}}.}}}}\geq b\ \ \}\ \ ||\ \ \{\ \langle\!\!\langle\mbox{{\texttt{a.key }}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\wedge\mbox{{\texttt{a.}}}\!\geq\!{\mbox{{\texttt{b}}}}\ \}

We use
S_{3}
, which says that
\ \rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{{$\mathbb{A}\!\!$}}{\mbox{{\texttt{a}}}:\mbox{{\texttt{Account}}},\mbox{{\texttt{b}}}:\mbox{{\texttt{int}}}}.{{\{}}{\langle\!\!\langle\mbox{{\texttt{a.key}}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\wedge\mbox{{\texttt{a.}}}\geq\mbox{{\texttt{b}}}}{{\}}}
.
We can apply rule
Call_Ext_Adapt
, by taking
y_{0}\triangleq\mbox{{\texttt{buyer}}}
, and
\overline{x:D}\triangleq\mbox{{\texttt{a}}}:\mbox{{\texttt{Account}}},\mbox{{\texttt{b}}}:\mbox{{\texttt{int}}}
,
and
A\triangleq\langle\!\!\langle\mbox{{\texttt{a.key}}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\wedge\mbox{{\texttt{a.}}}\geq\mbox{{\texttt{b}}}
, and
m\triangleq\mbox{{\texttt{pay}}}
, and
\overline{y}\triangleq\mbox{{\texttt{this.accnt}}},\mbox{{\texttt{price}}}
,
and provided that we can establish that

(2?)

{\small{\ \ \ \langle\!\!\langle\mbox{{\texttt{this.\mbox{{\texttt{accnt}}}.key}}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!(\mbox{{\texttt{buyer}}},\mbox{{\texttt{this.\mbox{{\texttt{accnt}}}}}},\mbox{{\texttt{price}}})}}

holds. Using type information, we obtain that all fields transitively accessible from
this.
accnt
.key
, or
price
are internal or scalar. This implies

(3)

{\small{\ \ \ \langle\!\!\langle\mbox{{\texttt{this.\mbox{{\texttt{accnt}}}.key}}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!\mbox{{\texttt{this.\mbox{{\texttt{accnt}}}}}}\wedge\langle\!\!\langle\mbox{{\texttt{this.\mbox{{\texttt{accnt}}}.key}}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!\mbox{{\texttt{price}}}}}

Using then Def.
6.1
, we can indeed establish that

(4)

{\small{\ \ \ \langle\!\!\langle\mbox{{\texttt{this.\mbox{{\texttt{accnt}}}.key}}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!(\mbox{{\texttt{buyer}}},\mbox{{\texttt{this.\mbox{{\texttt{accnt}}}}}},\mbox{{\texttt{price}}})\ =\ \langle\!\!\langle\mbox{{\texttt{this.\mbox{{\texttt{accnt}}}.key}}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!\mbox{{\texttt{buyer}}}}}
Then, by application of the rule of consequence,
(4)
, and the rule
Call_Ext_Adapt
, we can establish
(1)
. More details in §
H.3
.

6.3.
Third phase: Proving adherence to Module Specifications

In Fig.
9
we define the judgment
\vdash M
, which says that

M
has been proven to be well formed.

\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{array}[]{lcl}\begin{array}[]{c}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {{\mbox{~ {\sc{WellFrm\_Mod}}}}}\\
\vdash\mathscr{S}\!pec(M)\hskip 34.14322ptM\vdash\mathscr{S}\!pec(M)\\
\hline\cr\vdash M\end{array}&&\begin{array}[]{c}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {{\mbox{~ {\sc{Comb\_Spec}}}}}\\
M\vdash S_{1}\hskip 34.14322ptM\vdash S_{2}\\
\hline\cr M\vdash S_{1}\wedge S_{2}\end{array}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{c}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {{\mbox{~ \hskip 184.9429pt {\sc{method}}}}}\\
\mbox{{\texttt{mBody}}}(m,D,M)=p\ (\overline{y:D})\{\ stmt\ \}\\
{M\ \vdash\ \{\,\ \mbox{{\texttt{this}}}:\mbox{{\texttt{D}}},\overline{y:D}\,{\wedge\,A_{1}}\ \,\}\ stmt\ \{\,\ A_{2}\,{{\wedge\,A_{2}\!-\!\!\!\,\!{\triangledown}\mbox{{\texttt{res}}}}}\ \,\}\,\,\parallel\,\,\{\,A_{3}\,\}}\\
\hline\cr M\vdash{\{\,A_{1}\,\}\,p\ D\!::\!m(\overline{y:D})\,\{\,A_{2}\,\}\,\,\parallel\,\,\{A_{3}\}}\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{c}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {{\mbox{~ \hskip 184.9429pt {\sc{invariant}}}}}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\forall D,m:\ \ \ \ \ \mbox{{\texttt{mBody}}}(m,D,M)=\mbox{{\texttt{public}}}\ (\overline{y:D})\{\ stmt\ \}\ \ \Longrightarrow\\
~\hskip 8.5359pt\ \ \ \begin{array}[]{l}M\ \vdash\ \{\,\ \mbox{{\texttt{this}}}:\mbox{{\texttt{D}}},\overline{y:D},\,\overline{x:C}\,\wedge\,A\,{{\wedge\,A\!-\!\!\!\,\!{\triangledown}(\mbox{{\texttt{this}}},\overline{y})}}\,\,\}\ stmt\ \{\,\ A\,\wedge\,A\!-\!\!\!\,\!{\triangledown}\mbox{{\texttt{res}}}\ \,\}\,\,\parallel\,\,\{\,\ A\ \,\}\end{array}\end{array}\\
\hline\cr M\vdash\ \rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{{$\mathbb{A}\!\!$}}{\overline{x:C}}.{{\{}}{A}{{\}}}\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 9
.
Methods’ and Modules’ Adherence to Specification

WellFrm_Mod
and
Comb_Spec
say that
M
is well-formed if its specification is well-formed (according to Def.
5.6
), and if
M
satisfies all conjuncts of the specification.

Method
says that
a module satisfies a method specification if the body satisfies the corresponding pre-, post- and midcondition.
In the postcondition we also ask that
A\!-\!\!\!\,\!{\triangledown}\mbox{{\texttt{res}}}
, so that
res
does not leak any of the values that
A
promises will be protected.

Invariant
says that a module satisfies a specification
\ \rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{{$\mathbb{A}\!\!$}}{\overline{x:C}}.{{\{}}{A}{{\}}}
, if the method body of each public method
has
A
as its pre-, post- and midcondition.
Moreover, the precondition is strengthened by
A\!-\!\!\!\,\!{\triangledown}(\mbox{{\texttt{this}}},\overline{y})
– this is sound because
the caller is external, and by Lemma
6.2
, part (
4
).

Barendregt
In
method
we implicitly require the free variables in a method’s precondition not to overlap with variables in its body, unless they are the receiver or one of the parameters (
{{{\hskip-1.0ptV\hskip-1.0pt}s(stmt)\cap{\hskip-1.0ptF\hskip-1.0pt}v(A_{1})\subseteq\{\mbox{{\texttt{this}}},y_{1},...y_{n}\}}}
). And in
invariant
we require the free variables in
A
(which are a subset of
\overline{x}
) not to overlap with the variable in
stmt
(
{{{\hskip-1.0ptV\hskip-1.0pt}s(stmt)\,\cap\,\overline{x}\,=\,\emptyset}}
).
This can easily be achieved through renamings,
c.f.
Def.
F.1
.
