6.4.
Our Example Proven

Using our Hoare logic, we have developed a mechanised proof in Coq, that, indeed,
M_{good}\vdash S_{2}\wedge S_{3}
.
This proof is part of the current submission (in a
*.zip
file), and will be submitted as an artifact with the final version.

Our proof models
{\mathscr{L}}_{ul}
, the assertion language, the specification language, and the Hoare logic from §
6.1
, §
6.2
, §
6.3
, §
F
and Def.
6.1
.
In keeping with the start of §
6
, our proof assumes the existence of an underlying Hoare logic,
and several, standard, properties of that underlying logic, the assertions logic (
e.g.
equality of objects implies equality of field accesses) and of type systems
(
e.g.
fields of objects of different types cannot be aliases of one another).
All assumptions are clearly indicated in the associated artifact.

In appendix
H
, included in the auxiliary material, we outline the main ingredients of that proof.

7.
Soundness

We now give a synopsis of the proof of soundness of the logic from §
6
, and outline the
two most interesting aspects: deep satisfaction, and summarized execution.

Deep Satisfaction

We are faced with the problem that assertions are not always preserved when popping the top frame (
c.f.
Ex.
4.7
), while we need to be able to argue that method return preserves post-conditions.
For this, we introduce a “deeper” notion of assertion satisfaction, which requires that an assertion not only is satisfied from the viewpoint of the top frame, but also from the viewpoint of all frames from
k
-th frame onwards:
M,\sigma,{k}\models{A}
says that
\forall j.[\ k\!\leq\!j\leq\!|\sigma|\ \Rightarrow\ M,\sigma[j]\models A\ ]
.
Accordingly, we introduce
deep specification satisfaction
,
{\overline{M};M\models\!\!\!\!_{{}_{deep}}\ \,\{\,A\,\}\,\sigma\,\{\,A^{\prime}\,\}\,\parallel\,\{\,A^{\prime\prime}\,\}}
, which promises for all
k\!\leq\!|\sigma|
,
if
M,\sigma,{k}\models{A}
, and if scoped execution of
\sigma
’s continuation leads to final state
\sigma^{\prime}
and intermediate external state
\sigma^{\prime\prime}
, then

M,\sigma^{\prime},{k}\models{A^{\prime}}
, and
M,\sigma^{\prime\prime},{k}\models{A^{\prime\prime}}

-
c.f.
App.
G.3
.

Here how deep satisfaction addresses this problem: Assume state
\sigma_{1}
right before entering a call,
\sigma_{2}
and
\sigma_{3}
at start and end of the call’s body, and
\sigma_{4}
upon return. If a pre-condition holds at
\sigma_{1}
, then it holds for a
k\leq|\sigma_{1}|
; hence, if the postcondition holds for
k
at
\sigma_{3}
, and because
|\sigma_{3}|=|\sigma_{1}|\!+\!1
, it also holds for
\sigma_{4}
.
Deep satisfaction is stronger than shallow (
i.e.
specification satisfaction as in Def.
5.2
).

Lemma 7.1.

For all
\overline{M}
,
M
,
A
,
A^{\prime}
,
A^{\prime\prime}
,
\sigma
:

•

{\overline{M};M\models\!\!\!\!_{{}_{deep}}\ \,\{\,A\,\}\,\sigma\,\{\,A^{\prime}\,\}\,\parallel\,\{\,A^{\prime\prime}\,\}}\ \Longrightarrow\ \ {\overline{M};M\,\models\,\{\,A\,\}\,\sigma\,\{\,A^{\prime}\,\}\,\parallel\,\{\,A^{\prime\prime}\,\}}

Soundness of the Triples Logic

We require the assertion logic,
M\vdash A
, and the underlying Hoare logic,
M\ \vdash_{ul}\ \{\,A\,\}\ stmt\{\,A^{\prime}\,\}
, to be be sound.
Such sound logics do exist.
Namely, one can build an assertion logic,
M\vdash A
, by extending a logic which does not talk about protection, through the addition of structural rules which talk about protection; this extension preserves soundness -
c.f.
App.
G.1
.
Moreover, since the assertions
A
and
A^{\prime}
in
M\vdash_{ul}\ \{\,A\,\}\ stmt\{\,A^{\prime}\,\}
may, but need not, talk about protection,
one can take a Hoare logic from the literature as the
\vdash_{ul}
-logic.

We then prove soundness of the rules about protection from Fig.
7
, and, based on this,
we prove soundness of the inference system for triples –
c.f.
Appendix
G.5
.

Theorem 7.2.

For module
M
such that
\vdash M
, and for any assertions
A
,
A^{\prime}
,
A^{\prime\prime}
and statement
stmt
:

M\ \vdash\ \{\,A\,\}\ stmt\{\,A^{\prime}\,\}\ \ \ \ \Longrightarrow\ \ \ \ M\models\!\!\!\!_{{}_{deep}}\ \{\,A\,\}\ stmt\{\,A^{\prime}\,\}\,\,\parallel\,\,\{\,A^{\prime\prime}\,\}

Summarised Execution

Execution of an external call may consist of any number of external
transitions, interleaved with calls to public internal methods, which in
turn may make any number of further internal calls (public or private), and these, again may call external methods.
For the proof of soundness, internal and external transitions use different arguments.
For external transitions we consider small steps and argue in terms of preservation of encapsulated properties,
while for internal calls, we use large steps, and appeal to the method’s specification.
Therefore, we define
sumarized
executions, where internal calls are collapsed into one, large step,
e.g.
below:

summarized

to

Lemma
G.28
says that any terminating execution
starting in an external state consists of a sequence of external states interleaved with terminating executions
of public methods.
Lemma
G.29
says that such an execution preserves an encapsulated assertion
A

provided that all these finalising internal executions also preserve
A
.

Soundness of the Quadruples Logic

Proving soundness of our quadruples requires induction on the execution in some cases, and induction on the derivation of the quadruples in others. We address this through a well-founded ordering that combines both,
c.f.

Def.
G.22
and lemma
G.23
.
Finally, in
G.16
, we prove soundness:

Theorem 7.3.

For module
M
, assertions
A
,
A^{\prime}
,
A^{\prime\prime}
, state
\sigma
, and specification
S
:

(1)

:\ \vdash M\ \ \ \wedge\ \ \ M\ \vdash\ \{\,A\,\}\ stmt\{\,A^{\prime}\,\}\,\,\parallel\,\,\{\,A^{\prime\prime}\,\}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \Longrightarrow\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ M\ \models\!\!\!\!_{{}_{deep}}\ \ \{\,A\,\}\ stmt\{\,A^{\prime}\,\}\,\,\parallel\,\,\{\,A^{\prime\prime}\,\}

(2)

:\ \ M\ \vdash\ S\ \ \ \ \ \ \Longrightarrow\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {M}\models\!\!\!\!_{{}_{deep}}\ {S}

8.
Conclusion: Summary, Related Work and Further Work

Our motivation

comes from the OCAP approach to security, whereby object capabilities guard against un-sanctioned effects.
Miller
(
Miller et al. 2013
;
Miller 2006
)
advocates

defensive consistency
: whereby “An object is defensively
consistent when it can defend its own invariants and provide correct
service to its well behaved clients, despite arbitrary or malicious
misbehaviour by its other clients.” Defensively consistent
modules are hard to design and verify, but
make it much
easier to make guarantees about systems composed of multiple components

(
Murray 2010
)
.

Our Work
aims to elucidate such guarantees. We want to formalize and prove that

(
Drossopoulou et al. 2016
)
:

Lack of eventual access implies that certain properties will be preserved, even in the presence of external calls.

For this, we had to model the concept of lack of eventual access, determine the temporal scope of the preservation, and develop a Hoare logic framework to formally prove such guarantees.

For lack of eventual access, we introduced protection, a property of all the paths of all external objects accessible from the current stack frame.
For the temporal scope of preservation, we developed scoped invariants, which ensure that a given property holds as long as we have not returned from the current method.
(top of current stack has not been popped yet).
For our Hoare logic, we introduced an adaptation operator, which translates assertions between the caller’s and callee’s frames.
Finally, to prove the soundness of our approach, we developed the notion of deep satisfaction, which mandates that an assertion must be satisfied from a particular stack frame onward.
Thus, most concepts in this work are
scope-aware
, as they depend on the current stack frame.

With these concepts, we developed a specification language for modules limiting effects, a Hoare Logic for proving external calls, protection, and adherence to specifications, and have proven it sound.

Lack of Eventual Access
Efforts to restrict “eventual access” have been extensively explored, with
Ownership Types being a prominent example
(
Clarke et al. 2001
;
Cameron et al. 2013
)
.
These types enforce encapsulation boundaries to safeguard internal implementations, thereby ensuring representation independence and defensive consistency

(
Clarke et al. 1998
;
Noble et al. 1998
;
Banerjee and Naumann 2005
)
.
Ownership is fundamental to key systems like Rust’s memory safety

(
Klabnik and Nichols 2018
;
Jung et al. 2017
)
,
Scala’s Concurrency
(
Haller and Loiko 2016
;
Haller 2024
)
,
Java heap analyses
(
Potter et al. 1998
;
Hill et al. 2002
;
Mitchell 2006
)
,
and plays a critical role in program verification

(
Boyapati et al. 2003
;
Leinenbach and Santen 2009
)
including Spec
\#

(
Barnett et al. 2005
;
Barnett et al. 2004
)
and universes

(
Dietl et al. 2007b
;
Dietl and Müller 2005
;
Lu and Potter 2006
)
,
Borrowable Fractional Ownership
(
Nakayama et al. 2024
)
,
and recently integrated into languages like OCAML
(
Lorenzen et al. 2024
;
Marshall and Orchard 2024
)
.

Ownership types are closely related to the notion of protection: both are scoped relative to a frame. However, ownership requires an object to control some part of the path, while protection demands that module objects control the endpoints of paths.

In future work we want to explore how to express protection within Ownership Types, with the primary challenge being how to accommodate capabilities accessible to some external objects while still inaccessible to others.
Moreover, tightening some
rules in our current Hoare logic (e.g. Def.
4.4
)
may lead to a native Hoare logic of ownership.
Also, recent approaches like
the Alias
Calculus
(
Kogtenkov et al. 2015
;
Rivera and Meyer 2020
)
,
Reachability
Types
(
Bao et al. 2021
;
rompf-poly-reachability-oopsla2024
)

and Capturing
Types
(
Boruch-Gruszecki et al. 2023
;
Brachthäuser et al. 2022
;
Xu and Odersky 2024
)

abstract fine-grained method-level descriptions of
references and aliases flowing into and out of methods and fields,
and likely accumulate enough information to express
protection. Effect exclusion

(
Lutze et al. 2023
)
directly prohibits nominated
effects, but within a closed, fully-typed world.

Temporal scope of the guarantee
Starting with loop invariants
(
Hoare 1969
;
Floyd 1967
)
, property preservation at various granularities and durations has been widely and successfully adapted and adopted
(
Hoare 1974
;
Liskov and Wing 1994
;
Leino and Schulte 2007
;
Cohen et al. 2010
;
Meyer 1992b
;
Meyer 1992a
;
Barnett et al. 2004
;
Leino and Müller 2004
;
Müller et al. 2006
;
Drossopoulou et al. 2008
)
.
In our work, the temporal scope of the preservation guarantee includes all nested calls, until termination of the currently executing method, but not beyond.
We compare with object and history invariants in §
3.3.2
.

Such guarantees are maintained by the module as a whole.

Drossopoulou et al. 2020
proposed “holistic specifications” which take an external
perspective across the interface of a module.
Mackay et al. 2022
builds upon this work, offering a specification
language based on
necessary
conditions and temporal operators.
Neither of these systems support any kind of external calls.
Like
(
Drossopoulou et al. 2020
;
Mackay et al. 2022
)
we propose “holistic specifications”, albeit without temporal logics, and with sufficient conditions.
In addition, we introduce protection, and develop a Hoare logic for protection and external calls.

Hoare Logics
were first developed in Hoare’s seminal 1969 paper
(
Hoare 1969
)
, and have inspired a plethora of influential further developments and tools. We shall discuss a few only.

Separation logics
(
Ishtiaq and O’Hearn 2001
;
Reynolds 2002
)
reason about disjoint memory regions. Incorporating Separation Logic’s powerful framing mechanisms will pose several challenges:
We have no specifications and no footprint for external calls.
Because protection is “scope-aware”, expressing it as a predicate would require quantification over all possible paths and variables within the current stack frame.
We may also require a new separating conjunction operator.

Hyper-Hoare Logics
(
Dardinier and Müller 2024
;
D’Osualdo et al. 2022
)
reason about the execution of several programs, and could thus be applied to our problem, if extended to model all possible
sequences of calls of internal public methods.

Incorrectness Logic

(
O’Hearn 2019
)

under-approximates postconditions, and thus
reasons about the presence of bugs, rather than their absence.
Our work, like classical Hoare Logic, over-approximates postconditions,
and differs from Hoare and Incorrectness Logics
by tolerating interactions between verified code and unverified components.
Interestingly, even though earlier work in the space
(
Drossopoulou et al. 2020
;
Mackay et al. 2022
)
employ
necessary
conditions for effects (
i.e.
under-approximate pre-conditions), we can, instead, employ
sufficient
conditions for the lack of effects (over-approximate postconditions).
Incorporating our work into Incorrectness Logic might require under-approximating eventual access, while protection over-approximates it.

Rely-Guarantee

(
Hayes and Jones 2018
;
van Staden 2015
)

and Deny-Guarantee
(
Dodds et al. 2009
)
distinguish between assertions guaranteed by a thread, and those a
thread can reply upon.
Our Hoare quadruples are (roughly) Hoare triples plus
the “guarantee” portion of rely-guarantee.
When a
specification includes a guarantee, that guarantee must be maintained
by every “atomic step” in an execution

(
Hayes and Jones 2018
)
, rather than just at method
boundaries as in visible states semantics

(
Müller et al. 2006
;
Drossopoulou et al. 2008
;
Summers and Drossopoulou 2010
)
.
In concurrent reasoning,
this is because shared state may be accessed
by another coöperating thread at any time:
while in our case, it is because unprotected
state may be accessed by an untrusted component within the same
thread.

Models and Hoare Logics for the interaction with the the external world

Murray 2010
made the first attempt to formalise defensive
consistency, to tolerate interacting with any untrustworthy object,
although without a specification language for describing effects
(i.e. when an object is correct).

Cassez et al. 2024
propose one approach to reason about external calls.
Given that external callbacks are necessarily restricted to the module’s public interface,
external callsites are replaced with a
generated
externalcall()
method that nondeterministically invokes any method in that interface.

Rao et al. 2023b
’s Iris-Wasm is similar.
WASM’s
modules are very loosely coupled: a module
has its own byte memory
and object table.
Iris-Wasm ensures models
can only be
modified via their explicitly exported interfaces.

Swasey et al. 2017
designed OCPL, a logic
that separates internal implementations (“high values”)
from interface objects
(“low values”). OCPL supports defensive
consistency (called “robust safety” after the
security literature
(
Bengtson et al. 2011
)
)
by ensuring
low values can never leak high values, a and
prove object-capability patterns, such as
sealer/unsealer, caretaker, and membrane.
RustBelt
(
Jung et al. 2017
)

developed this approach to prove Rust memory safety using Iris
(
Jung et al. 2018
)
,
and combined with RustHorn
(
Matsushita et al. 2021
)
for the safe subset,
produced RustHornBelt
(
Matsushita et al. 2022
)
that verifies
both safe and unsafe Rust programs. Similar techniques were extended to C
(
Sammler et al. 2021
)
.
While these projects
verify “safe” and “unsafe” code,
the distinction is about memory safety:whereas all our code is ”memory safe”
but unsafe / untrusted code is unknown to the verifier.

Devriese et al. 2016
deploy step-indexing, Kripke worlds, and representing objects
as public/private state machines to model problems including the
DOM wrapper and a mashup application.
Their distinction between public and private transitions is similar to our
distinction between internal and external objects.
This stream of work has culminated in VMSL, an Iris-based separation logic for
virtual machines to assure defensive consistency

(
Liu et al. 2023
)

and Cerise, which uses Iris invariants to support proofs of programs with outgoing calls and callbacks,
on capability-safe CPUs
(
Georges et al. 2024
)
,
via problem-specific proofs in Iris’s logic.
Our work differs from Swasey, Schaefer’s, and Devriese’s work in that
they are primarily concerned with ensuring defensive consistency,
while we focus on module specifications.

Smart Contracts
also pose the problem of external calls.
Rich-Ethereum
(
Bräm et al. 2021
)

relies on Ethereum contracts’ fields being instance-private
and unaliased. Scilla
(
Sergey et al. 2019
)

is a minimalistic functional alternative to Ethereum,
which has demonstrated that popular Ethereum
contracts avoid common contract errors when using Scilla.

The VerX tool can verify
specifications for Solidity contracts automatically
(
Permenev et al. 2020
)
.
VerX’s specification language is based on temporal logic.
It is restricted to “effectively call-back free” programs

(
Grossman et al. 2018
;
Albert et al. 2023
)
,
delaying any callbacks until the
incoming call to the internal object has finished.

ConSol

(
Wei et al. 2024
)

provides a specification langauge for smart contracts,
checked at runtime
(
Findler and Felleisen 2001
)
.
SCIO*
(
Andrici et al. 2024
)
, implemented in
F*, supports both
verified and unverified code.
Both
Consol
and SCIO* are
similar to gradual verification techniques

(
Zimmerman et al. 2024
;
Cok and Leino 2022
)
that
insert dynamic checks between verified and unverified code,
and contracts for general access control

(
Dimoulas et al. 2014
;
Moore et al. 2016
;
Cutler et al. 2024
)
.

Programming languages with object capabilities

Google’s Caja
(
Miller et al. 2008
)
applies (object-)capabilities
(
Dennis and Horn 1966
;
Morris Jr. 1973
;
Miller 2006
)
,
sandboxes, proxies, and wrappers to limit components’
access to
ambient
authority.
Sandboxing has been validated
formally
(
Maffeis et al. 2010
)
;
Many recent languages
(
Hayes et al. 2017
;
Burtsev et al. 2017
;
Rhodes et al. 2014
)
including Newspeak

(
Bracha 2017
)
, Dart
(
Bracha 2015
)
, Grace
(
Black et al. 2012
;
Jones et al. 2016
)

and Wyvern
(
Melicher et al. 2017
)
have adopted object capabilities.

Schaefer et al. 2018
has also adopted
an information-flow approach to ensure confidentially by construction.

Anderson et al. 2023
extend memory safety arguments to “stack
safety”: ensuring method calls and returns are well bracketed (aka
“structured”), and that the integrity and confidentially of both
caller and callee are ensured, by assigning objects to security
classes.

Schaefer et al. 2018
has also adopted
an information-flow approach to ensure confidentially by construction.

Future work

We will look at the application of our techniques to
languages that rely on lexical nesting for access
control such as Javascript
(
Miller 2011
)
,
rather than public/private annotations,
languages that support ownership types such as Rust, leveraged for
verification

(
Astrauskas et al. 2019
;
Matsushita et al. 2022
;
Lattuada et al. 2023
)
,
and languages from the
functional tradition such as OCAML, with features such as ownership and uniqueness
(
Marshall and Orchard 2024
;
Lorenzen et al. 2024
)
.
These different language paradigms may lead us to refine our ideas for eventual access, footprints and framing operators.

We want to incorporate our techniques into existing program
verification tools
(
Cok and Leino 2022
)
, especially those attempting
gradual verification
(
Zimmerman et al. 2024
)
.

Appendix A
Appendix to Section
3
– The programming language
{\mathscr{L}}_{ul}

We introduce
{\mathscr{L}}_{ul}
, a simple, typed, class-based, object-oriented language.

A.1.
Syntax

The syntax of
{\mathscr{L}}_{ul}
is given in Fig.
4
13
13

13

Our motivating example is provided in a slightly richer syntax for greater readability.
.
To reduce the complexity of our formal models, as is usually done, CITE - CITE,
{\mathscr{L}}_{ul}
lacks many
common languages features, omitting static fields and methods, interfaces,
inheritance, subsumption, exceptions, and control flow.

{\mathscr{L}}_{ul}
and which may be defined recursively.

{\mathscr{L}}_{ul}
modules (
M
) map class names (
C
) to class definitions (
ClassDef
).
A class definition consists of a list of field definitions, ghost field definitions, and method definitions.
Fields, ghost fields, and methods all have types,
C
; types are
classes.
Ghost fields may be optionally
annotated as
intrnl
, requiring the argument to have an internal type, and the
body of the ghost field to only contain references to internal objects. This is enforced by the
limited type system of
{\mathscr{L}}_{ul}
.
A program state (
\sigma
) is a pair of of a stack and a heap.
The stack is a a stack is a non-empty list of frames (
\phi
), and the heal (
\chi
)
is a map from addresses (
\alpha
) to objects (
o
). A frame consists of a local variable
map and a continuation .
cont
that represents the statements that are yet to be executed (
s
).
A statement is either a field read (
x:=y.f
), a field write (
x.f:=y
), a method call
(
u:=y_{0}.m(\overline{y})
), a constructor call (
\mbox{{\texttt{new}}}\ C
),
a sequence of statements (
s;\ s
),
or empty (
\epsilon
).

{\mathscr{L}}_{ul}
also includes syntax for expressions
\mathit{e}
that may be used in writing
specifications or the definition of ghost fields.

A.2.
Semantics

{\mathscr{L}}_{ul}
is a simple object oriented language, and the operational semantics
(given in Fig.
5
and discussed later)
do not introduce any novel or surprising features. The operational
semantics make use of several helper definitions that we
define here.

We provide a definition of reference interpretation in Definition
A.1

Definition A.1.

For a frame
\phi=(\overline{x\mapsto v},s)
, and a program state
\sigma=(\overline{\phi}\cdot\phi,,\chi)
, we define:

•

\lfloor x\rfloor_{\phi}\ \triangleq\ v_{i}
if
x=x_{i}

•

\lfloor x\rfloor_{\sigma}\ \triangleq\ \lfloor x\rfloor_{\phi}

•

\lfloor\alpha.f\rfloor_{\sigma}\ \triangleq\ v_{i}
if
\chi(\alpha)=(\_;\ \overline{f\mapsto v})
, and
f_{i}=f

•

\lfloor x.f\rfloor_{\sigma}\ \triangleq\ \lfloor\alpha.f\rfloor_{\sigma}
where
\lfloor x\rfloor_{\sigma}=\alpha

•

\phi.\mbox{{\texttt{cont}}}\ \triangleq\ s

•

\sigma.\mbox{{\texttt{cont}}}\ \triangleq\ \phi.\mbox{{\texttt{cont}}}

•

\phi[\mbox{{\texttt{cont}}}\mapsto s^{\prime}]\ \triangleq\ (\overline{x\mapsto v},s^{\prime})

•

\sigma[\mbox{{\texttt{cont}}}\mapsto s^{\prime}]\ \triangleq\ (\ {\overline{\phi}}\cdot\phi[\mbox{{\texttt{cont}}}\mapsto s^{\prime}],\ \chi\ )

•

\phi[\mbox{{\texttt{x'}}}\mapsto v^{\prime}]\ \triangleq\ (\ (\overline{x\mapsto v})[\mbox{{\texttt{x'}}}\mapsto v^{\prime}],\ s\ )

•

\sigma[\mbox{{\texttt{x'}}}\mapsto v^{\prime}]\ \triangleq\ (\ (\overline{\phi}\cdot(\phi[\mbox{{\texttt{x'}}}\mapsto v^{\prime}]),\ \chi)

•

\sigma[\alpha\mapsto o]\ \triangleq\ (\ (\overline{\phi}\cdot\phi),\ \chi[\alpha\mapsto o]\ )

•

\sigma[\alpha.f^{\prime}\mapsto v^{\prime}]\ \triangleq\ \sigma[\alpha\mapsto o]
if
\chi(\alpha)=(C,{\overline{f\mapsto v}})
, and
o=(\ C;({\overline{f\mapsto v}})[f^{\prime}\mapsto v^{\prime}]\ )

That is, a variable
x
, or a field access on a variable
x.f

has an interpretation within a program state of value
v

if
x
maps to
v
in the local variable map, or the field

f
of the object identified by
x
points to
v
.

Definition
A.2
defines the class lookup function an object
identified by variable
x
.

Definition A.2 (Class Lookup).

For program state
\sigma=({\overline{\phi}}\cdot\phi,\chi)
, class lookup is defined as

\textit{classOf}(\sigma,x)\ \triangleq\ C\ \ \ \ \ \mbox{if}\ \ \ \chi(\lfloor x\rfloor_{\sigma})=(C,\_)

Module linking is defined for modules with disjoint definitions:

Definition A.3.

For all modules
\overline{M}
and
M
, if the domains of
\overline{M}
and
M
are disjoint,
we define the module linking function as
M\cdot\overline{M}\ \triangleq\ M\ \cup\ M^{\prime}
.

That is, their linking is the union of the two if their domains are disjoint.

Definition
A.4
defines the method lookup function for a method
call
m
on an object of class
C
.

Definition A.4 (Method Lookup).

For module
\overline{M}
, class
C
, and method name
m
, method lookup is defined as

\textit{Meth}(\overline{M},C,m)\ \triangleq\ {pr}\ \mbox{{\texttt{method}}}\ m\ (\overline{x:T}){:T}\{\ s\ \}

if there exists an
M
in
\overline{M}
, so that
M(C)
contains the definition
{pr}\ \mbox{{\texttt{method}}}\ m\ (\overline{x:T}){:T}\{\ s\ \}

Definition
A.5
looks up all the field identifiers in a given class

Definition A.5 (Fields Lookup).

For modules
\overline{M}
,and class
C
, fields lookup is defined as

fields(\overline{M},C)\ \triangleq\ \ \{\ f\ |\ \exists M\in\overline{M}.s.t.M(C)\mbox{contains the definition}\mbox{{\texttt{field}}}\ f:T\ \}

We define what it means for two objects to come from the same module

Definition A.6 (Same Module).

For program state
\sigma
, modules
\overline{M}
, and variables
x
and
y
, we defone

{SameModule(x,y,\sigma,\overline{M})}\ \triangleq\ \exists C,C^{\prime},M[\ M\in\overline{M}\wedge C,C^{\prime}\in M\wedge\textit{classOf}(\sigma,x)=C\wedge\textit{classOf}(\sigma,y)=C^{\prime}\ ]

As we already said in §
3.6
, we forbid assignments to a method’s parameters.
To do that, the following function returns the identifiers of the formal parameters of the currently active method.

Definition A.7.

For program state
\sigma
:

Prms(\sigma,\overline{M})\ \ \triangleq\ \ \overline{x}\ \ \ \mbox{such that}\ \ \ \exists\overline{\phi},\,\phi_{k},\,\phi_{k+1},\,C,\,p.

\hskip 91.04872pt[\ \ \sigma=(\overline{\phi}\cdot{\phi_{k}}\cdot{\phi_{k+1}}\,,\chi)\ \ \wedge\ \ \phi_{k}.\mbox{{\texttt{cont}}}=\_:=y_{0}.m(\_);\_\ \ \wedge\ \

\hskip 91.04872pt\textit{classOf}((\phi_{k+1},\chi),\mbox{{\texttt{this}}})\ \ \wedge\ \ \textit{Meth}(\overline{M},C,m)=p\ C::m(\overline{x:\_}){:\_}\{\_\}\ ]

While the small-step operational semantics of
{\mathscr{L}}_{ul}
is given in Fig.
5
,
specification satisfaction is defined over an abstracted notion of
the operational semantics that models the open world.

An
Initial
program state contains a single frame
with a single local variable
this
pointing to a single object
in the heap of class
Object
, and a continuation.

Definition A.8 (Initial Program State).

A program state
\sigma
is said to be an initial state (
\textit{Initial}(\sigma)
)
if and only if

•

\sigma\ =\ (((\mbox{{\texttt{this}}}\ \mapsto\ \alpha),s);\ (\alpha\mapsto(\mbox{{\texttt{Object}}},\emptyset)

for some address
\alpha
and some statement
s
.

We provide a semantics for expression evaluation is given in Fig.
10
.
That is, given a module
M
and a program state
\sigma
, expression
e
evaluates to
v

if
M,\sigma,e\hookrightarrow v
. Note, the evaluation of expressions is separate from the operational
semantics of
{\mathscr{L}}_{ul}
, and thus there is no restriction on field access.

Lemmas and Proofs

Proof of lemma
3.6

The first assertion is proven by unfolding the definition of
\_\models\_
.

The second assertion is proven by case analysis on the execution relation
\_,\sigma\dashrightarrow\ \sigma^{\prime}
.
The assertion gets established when we call a method, and is preserved through all the execution steps, because we do not allow assignments to the formal parameters.

End Proof

We now prove lemma
B.2
:

Proof of lemma
B.2

•

We first show that
(\overline{M},\sigma_{sc});\,{\sigma}\rightsquigarrow\sigma^{\prime}\ \wedge\ k<|\sigma|_{sc}\ \ \Longrightarrow\ \ \lfloor y\rfloor_{\sigma[k]}=\lfloor y\rfloor_{\sigma^{\prime}[k]}

This follows easily from the operational semantics, and the definitions.

•

By induction on the earlier part, we obtain that
\overline{M};\,{\sigma}{\rightsquigarrow^{*}}\sigma^{\prime}\ \wedge\ k<|\sigma|\ \ \Longrightarrow\ \ \lfloor y\rfloor_{\sigma[k]}=\lfloor y\rfloor_{\sigma^{\prime}[k]}

•

We now show that
\overline{M};\,{\sigma}{\rightsquigarrow^{*}}\!\!_{\mathit{fin}}\,\sigma^{\prime}\ \wedge\ y\notin{\hskip-1.0ptV\hskip-1.0pt}s(\sigma.\mbox{{\texttt{cont}}})\ \ \Longrightarrow\ \ \lfloor y\rfloor_{\sigma}=\lfloor y\rfloor_{\sigma^{\prime}}
by induction on the number of steps, and using the earlier lemma.

End Proof

states that initila states are well-formed, and that (
2

Appendix B
Appendix to Section
3.3
– Fundamental Concepts

Lemma
B.1
says, essentially, that scoped executions describe the same set of executions as those starting at an initial state
14
14

14

An
Initial
state’s heap contains a single object of class
Object
, and
its stack consists of a single frame, whose local variable map is a mapping from
this
to the single object, and whose continuation is any statement.
(See Def.
A.8
)
.
For instance, revisit Fig.
3
, and assume that
\sigma_{6}
is an initial state.
We have
\overline{M};\sigma_{10}\!\dashrightarrow^{*}\!{\sigma_{14}}
and
\overline{M};\,{\sigma_{10}}\not\!\!\!{\rightsquigarrow^{*}}\sigma_{14}
, but also

\overline{M};\,{\sigma_{6}}{\rightsquigarrow^{*}}\sigma_{14}
.

Lemma B.1.

For all modules
\overline{M}
, state
\sigma_{init}
,
\sigma
,
\sigma^{\prime}
, where

\sigma_{init}
is initial:

•

\overline{M};\,{\sigma}{\rightsquigarrow^{*}}\sigma^{\prime}\ \ \Longrightarrow\ \ \overline{M};\sigma\!\dashrightarrow^{*}\!{\sigma^{\prime}}

•

\overline{M};\sigma_{init}\!\dashrightarrow^{*}\!{\sigma^{\prime}}\ \ \Longrightarrow\ \ \overline{M};\,{\sigma_{init}}{\rightsquigarrow^{*}}\sigma^{\prime}
.

Lemma
B.2
says that scoped execution does not affect the contents of variables in earlier frames.
and that
the interpretation of a variable remains unaffected by
scoped execution of statements which do not mention that variable. More in Appendix
B
.

Lemma B.2.

For any modules
\overline{M}
, states
\sigma
,
\sigma^{\prime}
, variable
y
, and number
k
:

•

\overline{M};\,{\sigma}{\rightsquigarrow^{*}}\sigma^{\prime}\ \wedge\ k<|\sigma|\ \ \Longrightarrow\ \ \lfloor y\rfloor_{\sigma[k]}=\lfloor y\rfloor_{\sigma^{\prime}[k]}

•

Proof of lemma
B.2

•

We unfolding the definition of
\overline{M};\,{\sigma}\rightsquigarrow\sigma^{\prime}

\overline{M};\,{\sigma}\rightsquigarrow\sigma^{\prime}
and the rules of the operational semantics.

•

Take
k=|\sigma|
.
We unfold the definition from
3.2
, and obtain that

\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}
or,
\exists\sigma_{1},...\sigma_{n1}.\forall i\!\in\![1..n)[\ \overline{M};\sigma_{i}\!\dashrightarrow\!{\sigma_{i+1}}\ \wedge\ |\sigma_{1}|\leq|\sigma_{i+1}|\ \wedge\sigma=\sigma_{1}\ \wedge\ \sigma^{\prime}=\sigma_{n}\ ]

Consider the second case.
Take any
i\!\in\![1..n)
. Then, by Definition,
k\leq|\sigma|
.
If
k=|\sigma_{i}|
, then we are executing part of
\sigma.prg{cont}
, and because

y\notin{\hskip-1.0ptV\hskip-1.0pt}s(\sigma.\mbox{{\texttt{cont}}})
, we get
\lfloor y\rfloor_{\sigma[i]}=\lfloor y\rfloor_{\sigma_{i+1}[k]}
.
If
k=|\sigma_{i}|
, then we apply the bullet from above, and also obtain

\lfloor y\rfloor_{\sigma[i]}=\lfloor y\rfloor_{\sigma_{i+1}[k]}

This gives that
\lfloor y\rfloor_{\sigma[k]}=\lfloor y\rfloor_{\sigma^{\prime}[k]}
.
Moreover, because
\overline{M};\,{\sigma}{\rightsquigarrow^{*}}\!\!_{\mathit{fin}}\,\sigma^{\prime}
we obtain that

|\sigma|=|\sigma^{\prime}|=k
.
Therefore, we have that
\lfloor y\rfloor_{\sigma}=\lfloor y\rfloor_{\sigma^{\prime}}
.

End Proof

We also prove that in well-formed states (
\models\sigma
), all objects locally reachable from a given frame also locally reachable from the frame below.

Lemma B.3.

\models\sigma\ \ \wedge k<|\sigma|\ \ \ \ \Longrightarrow\ \ \ \ LocRchbl(\sigma[k+1])\subseteq LocRchbl(\sigma[k])

Proof.

By unfolding the definitions: Everything that is in
\sigma[k+1]
is reachable from its frame, and everything that is reachable from the frame of
\sigma[k+1]
is also reachable from the frame of
\sigma[k]
. We then apply that
\models\sigma

∎

Proof of lemma
3.6

(1)

By unfolding and folding the definitions.
Namely, everything that is locally reachable in
\sigma^{\prime}
is locally reachable through the frame
\phi
, and everything in the frame
\phi
is locally reachable in
\sigma
.

(2)

We require that
\models\sigma
– as we said earlier, we require this implicitly.
Here we apply induction on the execution.
Each step is either a method call (in which case we apply the bullet from above), or a return statement (then we apply lemma
B.3
), or the creation of a new object (in which case reachable set is the same as that from previous state plus the new object),
or an assignment to a variable (in which case the locally reachable objects in the new state are a subset of the locally reachable from the old state), or a an assignment to a field.
In the latter case, the locally reachable objects are also a subset of the locally reachable objects from the previous state.

End Proof

In order to prove
4.5
from the next appendix, we first formulate and prove the following auxiliary lemma, which allows us to replace any variable
x
in an extended expression
\mathit{e}
, by its interpretation

Lemma C.1.

For all extended expressions
\mathit{e}
, addresses
\alpha
and variables
x
, so that
x\in dom(\sigma):

•

M,\sigma,{\mathit{e}}\hookrightarrow\alpha\ \ \ \Longleftrightarrow\ \ \ \ M,\sigma,{\mathit{e}[{\lfloor x\rfloor_{\sigma}}/x]}\hookrightarrow\alpha

Note that in the above we require that
x\in dom(\sigma)
, in order to ensure that the replacement
[{\lfloor x\rfloor_{\sigma}}/x]
is well-defined.
On the other hand,
we do not require that
x\in{\hskip-1.0ptF\hskip-1.0pt}v(\mathit{e})
, because
if
x\notin{\hskip-1.0ptF\hskip-1.0pt}v(\mathit{e})
, then
{\mathit{e}[{\lfloor x\rfloor_{\sigma}}/x]}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny txt}}}}{{=}}}\mathit{e}
and the guarantee from above becomes a tautology.

– as defined in Def.

– and according to the expression evaluation rules from Fig.

D.1.
Stability

We first give complete definitions for the concepts of
{Stbl(\_])}
and
{Stb^{+}(\_)}

Definition D.2.

[
{Stbl(\_)}
] assertions:

\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{array}[]{c}{Stbl(\langle\!\!\langle\mathit{e}\rangle\!\!\rangle)}\triangleq false\\
{Stbl(\langle\!\!\langle\mathit{e}\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!\overline{u})}={Stbl(\mathit{e}:\mbox{{\texttt{intl}}})}={Stbl(\mathit{e})}={Stbl(\mathit{e}:C)}\ \triangleq\ true\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{lcl}{Stbl(A_{1}\wedge A_{2})}\ \triangleq\ {Stbl(A_{1})}\wedge{Stbl(A_{2})}&&{Stbl(\forall x:C.A)}={Stbl(\neg A)}\ \triangleq\ {Stbl(A)}\end{array}\end{array}

Definition D.3 (
{Stb^{+}(\_)}
).

assertions:

\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{array}[]{c}{Stb^{+}(\langle\!\!\langle\mathit{e}\rangle\!\!\rangle)}={Stb^{+}(\langle\!\!\langle\mathit{e}\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!\overline{u})}={Stb^{+}(\mathit{e}:\mbox{{\texttt{intl}}})}={Stb^{+}(\mathit{e})}={Stb^{+}(\mathit{e}:C)}\ \triangleq\ true\\
{Stb^{+}(A_{1}\wedge A_{2})}\ \triangleq\ {Stb^{+}(A_{1})}\wedge{Stb^{+}(A_{2})}\\
{Stb^{+}(\forall x:C.A)}\triangleq\ {Stb^{+}(A)}\\
{Stb^{+}(\neg A)}\triangleq{Stbl(A)}\end{array}\end{array}

The definition of
{Stb^{+}(\_)}
is less general than would be possible.
E.g.,

(\langle\!\!\langle x\rangle\!\!\rangle\rightarrow x.f=4)\rightarrow xf.3=7
does not satisfy our definition of
{Stb^{+}(\_)}
.
We have given these less general definitions in order to simplify both our defintions and our proofs.

Proof of lemma
4.6

Take any state
\sigma
, frame
\phi
, assertion
A
,

•

To show

{Stbl(A)}\ \wedge\ {\hskip-1.0ptF\hskip-1.0pt}v(A)=\emptyset\ \ \ \Longrightarrow\ \ \ \ [\ \ M,\sigma\models A\ \ \Longleftrightarrow\ \ M,{\sigma\,{\triangledown}\,\phi}\models A\ \ ]

By induction on the structure of the definition of
{Stbl(A)}
.

•

To show

{Stb^{+}(A)}\ \wedge\ {\hskip-1.0ptF\hskip-1.0pt}v(A)=\emptyset\ \wedge\ {M\cdot\overline{M}\models{\sigma\,{\triangledown}\,\phi}}\ \wedge\ \ M,\sigma\models A\ \wedge\ M,{\sigma\,{\triangledown}\,\phi}\models\mbox{{\texttt{intl}}}\ \ \Longrightarrow

\hskip 56.9055pt\ \ M,{\sigma\,{\triangledown}\,\phi}\models A

By induction on the structure of the definition of
{Stb^{+}(A)}
.
The only interesting case is when
A
has the form
\langle\!\!\langle\mathit{e}\rangle\!\!\rangle
. Because

fv(A)=\emptyset
, we know that
\lfloor\mathit{e}\rfloor_{\sigma}
=
\lfloor\mathit{e}\rfloor_{\sigma\,{\triangledown}\,\phi}
. Therefore, we assume that

\lfloor\mathit{e}\rfloor_{\sigma}=\alpha
for some
\alpha
, assume that
M,\sigma\models\langle\!\!\langle\alpha\rangle\!\!\rangle
, and want to show that
M,{\sigma\,{\triangledown}\,\phi}\models\langle\!\!\langle\alpha\rangle\!\!\rangle
.
From
{M\cdot\overline{M}\models{\sigma\,{\triangledown}\,\phi}}
we obtain that

Rng(\phi)\subseteq Rng(\sigma)
.
From this, we obtain that

LocRchbl(\sigma\,{\triangledown}\,\phi)\subseteq LocRchbl(\sigma)
.
The rest follows by unfolding and folding Def.
4.4
.

End Proof

D.2.
Encapsulation

Proofs of adherence to
{\mathscr{L}}{{}^{{spec}}}
specifications hinge on the expectation that some,
specific, assertions cannot be invalidated unless some
internal (and thus known) computation took place.
We call such assertions
encapsulated
.

We define the judgement,
M\ \vdash{Enc(A)}
, in terms of the judgment
M;\Gamma\vdash{Enc(A)};\Gamma^{\prime}
from Fig.
14
.
This judgements ensures that any objects whose fields are read in the validation of
A
are internal,
that
\langle\!\!\langle\_\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!\_
does not appear in
A
, and that protection assertions (ie
\langle\!\!\langle\rangle\!\!\rangle
or
\langle\!\!\langle\_\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!\_
) do not appear in negative positions in
A
. The second environment in this judgement,
\Gamma^{\prime}
, is used to keep track of any variables introduces in that judgment,
e.g.
we would have that

\hskip 28.45274ptM_{good},\emptyset\ \vdash\ {Enc(a:\mbox{{\texttt{Account}}}\wedge k:\mbox{{\texttt{Key}}}\wedge\langle\!\!\langle a.\mbox{{\texttt{key}}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\wedge a.\mbox{{\texttt{key}}}\neq k)};\ (a:\mbox{{\texttt{Account}}},k:\mbox{{\texttt{Key}}}
.

We assume a type judgment
M;\Gamma\vdash e:\mbox{{\texttt{intl}}}
which says that in the context of
\Gamma
, the expression
e
belongs to a class from
M
.
We also assume that the judgement
M;\Gamma\vdash e:\mbox{{\texttt{intl}}}
can deal with ghostfields – namely, ghost-methods have to be type checked in the contenxt of
M
and therefor they will only read the state of internal objects.
Note that it is possible for
M;\Gamma\vdash{Enc(\mathit{e})};\Gamma^{\prime}
to hold and

M;\Gamma\vdash e:\mbox{{\texttt{intl}}}
not to hold –
c.f.
rule
Enc_1
.

\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{array}[]{lclcl}\begin{array}[]{c}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {{\mbox{~ {\sc{Enc\_1}}}}}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}M;\Gamma\vdash\mathit{e}:\mbox{{\texttt{intl}}}\\
M;\Gamma\vdash{Enc(\mathit{e})};\ \Gamma^{\prime}\end{array}\\
\hline\cr M;\Gamma\vdash{Enc(\mathit{e}.f)};\ \Gamma^{\prime}\end{array}&&\begin{array}[]{c}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {{\mbox{~ {\sc{Enc\_2}}}}}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}\\
M;\Gamma\vdash{Enc(\mathit{e})};\ \Gamma^{\prime}\end{array}\\
\hline\cr M;\Gamma\vdash{Enc(\mathit{e}:C)};\ (\Gamma^{\prime},\mathit{e}:C)\end{array}&&\begin{array}[]{c}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {{\mbox{~ {\sc{Enc\_3}}}}}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}M;\Gamma\vdash{Enc(A)};\ \Gamma^{\prime}\\
A\mbox{\ does\ not\ contain\ $\langle\!\!\langle\_\rangle\!\!\rangle$}\end{array}\\
\hline\cr M;\Gamma\vdash{Enc(\neg A)};\ \Gamma^{\prime}\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {{\mbox{~ {\sc{Enc\_4}}}}}\\
\begin{array}[]{l}M;\Gamma\vdash{Enc(A_{1})};\ \Gamma^{\prime\prime}\\
M;\Gamma^{\prime\prime}\vdash{Enc(A_{2})};\ \Gamma^{\prime}\end{array}\\
\hline\cr M;\Gamma\vdash{Enc(A_{1}\wedge A_{2})};\ \Gamma^{\prime}\end{array}&&\begin{array}[]{c}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {{\mbox{~ {\sc{Enc\_5}}}}}\\
\\
M;\Gamma,{x:C}\vdash{Enc(A)};\ \Gamma^{\prime}\\
\hline\cr M;\Gamma\vdash{Enc(\forall{x:C}.A)};\ \Gamma^{\prime}\end{array}&&\begin{array}[]{c}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {{\mbox{~ {\sc{Enc\_6}}}}}\\
\\
M;\Gamma\vdash{Enc(\mathit{e})};\ \Gamma^{\prime}\\
\hline\cr M;\Gamma\vdash{Enc(\mathit{e}:\mbox{{\texttt{extl}}})};\ \Gamma^{\prime}\end{array}\end{array}\\
\\
\begin{array}[]{c}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ {{\mbox{~ {\sc{Enc\_7}}}}}\\
M;\Gamma\vdash{Enc(\mathit{e})};\Gamma^{\prime}\\
\hline\cr M;\Gamma\vdash{Enc(\langle\!\!\langle\mathit{e}\rangle\!\!\rangle)};\ \Gamma^{\prime}\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 14
.
The judgment
M;\Gamma\vdash{Enc(A)};\Gamma^{\prime}

Definition D.4 (An assertion
A
is
encapsulated
by module
M
).

•

M\vdash{Enc(A)}\ \ \triangleq\ \ \exists\Gamma.[\ M;\emptyset\vdash{Enc(A)};\Gamma\ ]
as defined in Fig.
14
.

To motivate the design of our judgment
M;\Gamma\vdash{Enc(A)};\Gamma^{\prime}
, we first give a semantic notion of encapsulation:

Definition D.5.

An assertion
A
is semantically encapsulated by module
M
:

•

M\ \models{Enc(A)}\ \ \triangleq\ \forall\overline{M},\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}.[\ \ M,\sigma\models(A\wedge\mbox{{\texttt{extl}}})\ \wedge\ {M\!\cdot\!\overline{M};\,{\sigma}\rightsquigarrow\sigma^{\prime}}\ \Longrightarrow\ {M},{\sigma^{\prime}}\models{{}^{\,\sigma}\!\lceil A\rceil}\ \ ]

More on Def.
D.5
If the definition
D.5
or in lemma
4.8
we had used the more general execution,
M\!\cdot\!\overline{M};\sigma\!\dashrightarrow\!{\sigma^{\prime}}
, rather than the scoped execution,
M\!\cdot\!\overline{M};\,{\sigma}\rightsquigarrow\sigma^{\prime}
,
then fewer assertions would have been encapsulated.
Namely, assertions like
\langle\!\!\langle x.f\rangle\!\!\rangle
would not be encapsulated.
Consider,
e.g.
, a heap
\chi
, with objects
1
,
2
,
3
and
4
, where
1
,
2
are external, and
3
,
4
are internal, and
1
has fields pointing to
2
and
4
, and
2
has a field pointing to
3
, and
3
has a field
f
pointing to
4
. Take state
\sigma
=
(\phi_{1}\!\cdot\!\phi_{2},\chi)
, where
\phi_{1}
’s receiver is
1
,
\phi_{2}
’s receiver is
2
, and there are no local variables.
We have
...\sigma\models\mbox{{\texttt{extl}}}\wedge\langle\!\!\langle 3.f\rangle\!\!\rangle
.
We return from the most recent all,
getting
...;\sigma\!\dashrightarrow\!{\sigma^{\prime}}
where
\sigma^{\prime}=(\phi_{1},\chi)
; and have
...,\sigma^{\prime}\not\models\langle\!\!\langle 3.f\rangle\!\!\rangle
.

Example D.6.

For an assertion
A_{bal}\ \triangleq\ a:\mbox{{\texttt{Account}}}\wedge a.\mbox{{\texttt{balance}}}=b
, and modules
M
{}_{{\textit{bad}}}
and
M
{}_{{\textit{fine}}}
from §
2
, we have
\mbox{{\texttt{M}}}_{{\textit{bad}}}\ \models\ {Enc(A_{bal})}
, and
\mbox{{\texttt{M}}}_{{\textit{bad}}}\ \models\ {Enc(A_{bal})}
.

Example D.7.

Assume further modules,
\mbox{{\texttt{M}}}_{{unp}}
and
\mbox{{\texttt{M}}}_{{prt}}
, which use ledgers mapping accounts to their balances, and export functions that update this map. In
\mbox{{\texttt{M}}}_{{unp}}
the ledger is part of the internal module, while in
\mbox{{\texttt{M}}}_{{prt}}
it is part of the external module.
Then
\mbox{{\texttt{M}}}_{{unp}}\ \not\models{Enc(A_{bal})}
, and
\mbox{{\texttt{M}}}_{{prt}}\models{Enc(A_{bal})}
.
Note that in both
\mbox{{\texttt{M}}}_{{unp}}
and
\mbox{{\texttt{M}}}_{{prt}}
, the term
a.balance
is a ghost field.

Note D.8.

Relative protection is not encapsulated, (
e.g.

M\not\models{{Enc(\langle\!\!\langle x\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!y)}}
), even though absolute protection is
(
e.g.

M\models{Enc(\langle\!\!\langle x\rangle\!\!\rangle)}
).
Encapsulation of an assertion does not imply encapsulation of its negation;
for example,
M\not\models{{Enc(\neg\langle\!\!\langle x\rangle\!\!\rangle)}}
.

More on Def.
D.4
This definition is less permissive than necessary.
For example
M\not\vdash{Enc(\neg({\neg\langle\!\!\langle x\rangle\!\!\rangle}))}
even though

M\models{Enc(\neg({\neg\langle\!\!\langle x\rangle\!\!\rangle}))}
.
Namely,

\neg(\neg\langle\!\!\langle x\rangle\!\!\rangle)\equiv\langle\!\!\langle x\rangle\!\!\rangle
and
M\vdash{{Enc(\langle\!\!\langle x\rangle\!\!\rangle)}}
.
A more permissive, sound, definition, is not difficult, but not the main aim of this work.
We gave this, less permissive definition, in order to simplify the definitions and the proofs.

Proof of lemma
4.8
This says that
M\vdash{{Enc(A)}}
implies that
M\vdash{{Enc(A)}}
.

We fist prove that

(*) Assertions
A_{poor}
which do not contain
\langle\!\!\langle\_\rangle\!\!\rangle
or
\langle\!\!\langle\_\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!\_
are preserved by any external step.

Namely, such an assertion only depends on the contents of the fields of internal objects, and these are not modified by external steps.
Such an
A_{poor}
is defined essentially through

\begin{array}[]{lllr}\ \ \ \ A_{poor}&::=&{\mathit{e}}\;\;|\;\;{\mathit{e}}:C\;\;|\;\;\neg A_{poor}\;\;|\;\;A_{poor}\ \wedge\ A_{poor}\;\;|\;\;\forall x:C.A_{poor}\;\;|\;\;{\mathit{e}}:\mbox{{\texttt{extl}}}&\text{\bf{}}\\
\\
\end{array}

We can prove (*) by induction on the structure of
A_{poor}
and case analysis on the execution step.

We then prove Lemma
4.8
by induction on the structure of
A
.

— The cases
Enc_1
,
Enc_2
, and
Enc_6

are straight application of (*).

— The case
Enc_3
also follows from (*), because any
A
which satisfies
{Enc(A)}
and which does not contain
\langle\!\!\langle\_\rangle\!\!\rangle

is an
A_{poor}
assertion.

— The cases
Enc_4
and
Enc_5
follow by induction hypothesis.

— The case
Enc_7
is more interesting.

We assume that
\sigma
is an external state, that
...;\,{\sigma}\rightsquigarrow\sigma^{\prime}
, and that
..\sigma\models\langle\!\!\langle\mathit{e}\rangle\!\!\rangle
.
By definition, the latter means that

(**) no locally reachable external object in
\sigma
has a field ponting to
\mathit{e}
,

nor is
\mathit{e}
one of the variables.

We proceed by case analysis on the step
...;\,{\sigma}\rightsquigarrow\sigma^{\prime}
.

- If that step was an assignment to a local variable
x
, then this does not affect
\langle\!\!\langle^{\,\sigma}\!\lceil\mathit{e}\rceil\rangle\!\!\rangle
because in
\lfloor\mathit{e}\rfloor_{\sigma}
=
\lfloor^{\,\sigma}\!\lceil\mathit{e}\rceil\rfloor_{\sigma^{\prime}}
,
and
...\sigma^{\prime}\models x\neq re
.

- If that step was an assignment to an external object’s field, of the form
x.f:=y
then this does not affect
\langle\!\!\langle^{\,\sigma}\!\lceil\mathit{e}\rceil\rangle\!\!\rangle
either.
This is so, because
{Enc(\mathit{e})}
gives that
\lfloor\mathit{e}\rfloor_{\sigma}
=
\lfloor^{\prime}\rfloor_{\sigma}{{}^{\,\sigma}\!\lceil\mathit{e}\rceil}
– namely the evaluation of
\mathit{e}
does not read
x
’s fields, since
x
is external.
And moreover, the assignment
x.f:=y
cannot create a new, unprotected path to
\mathit{e}
(unprotected means here that the penultimate element in that path is external), because then we would have had in
\sigma
an unprotected path from
y
to
\mathit{e}
.

- If that step was a method call, then we apply lemma
3.6
which says that all objects reachable in
\sigma^{\prime}
were already reachable in
\sigma
.

- Finally, we do not consider method return (
i.e.
the rule
Return
), because we are looking at
...;\,{\_}\rightsquigarrow\_
execution steps rather than
...;\_\!\dashrightarrow\!{\_}
steps.

End Proof

Appendix to Section
5
– Specifications

Example E.1 (Badly Formed Method Specifications).

S_{9,bad\_1}
is not a well-formed specification, because
A^{\prime}
is not a formal parameter, nor free in the precondition.

{\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ S_{9,bad\_1}}

\triangleq

\{\,a:\mbox{{\texttt{Account}}}\wedge\langle\!\!\langle a\rangle\!\!\rangle\,\}\,

\ \ \ \ \ \ \mbox{{\texttt{public Account}}}::\mbox{{\texttt{set}}}({\mbox{{\texttt{key'}}}:\mbox{{\texttt{Key}}}})

\{\,\langle\!\!\langle a\rangle\!\!\rangle\wedge\langle\!\!\langle a^{\prime}.\mbox{{\texttt{key}}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\,\}\ {\,\parallel\,\ \{true\}}

{\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ S_{9,bad\_2}}

\triangleq

\{\,a:\mbox{{\texttt{Account}}}\wedge\langle\!\!\langle a\rangle\!\!\rangle\,\}\,

\ \ \ \ \ \ \mbox{{\texttt{public Account}}}::\mbox{{\texttt{set}}}({\mbox{{\texttt{key'}}}:\mbox{{\texttt{Key}}}})

\{\,\langle\!\!\langle a\rangle\!\!\rangle\wedge\langle\!\!\langle a^{\prime}.\mbox{{\texttt{key}}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\,\}\ {\,\parallel\,\ \{\mbox{{\texttt{this}}}.\ \}}

Example E.2 (More Method Specifications).

S_{7}
below guarantees that

transfer
does not affect the balance of accounts different from the receiver or argument, and if the key supplied is not that of the receiver, then no account’s balance is affected.
S_{8}
guarantees that if the key supplied is that of the receiver, the correct amount is transferred from the receiver to the destination.

S_{9}
guarantees that
set
preserves the protectedness of a key.

\triangleq

\{\,a:\mbox{{\texttt{Account}}}\wedge a.\mbox{{\texttt{}}}=b\wedge(\mbox{{\texttt{dst}}}\neq a\neq\mbox{{\texttt{this}}}\vee\mbox{{\texttt{key'}}}\neq a.\mbox{{\texttt{key }}})\,\}\,

\ \ \ \ \ \ \mbox{{\texttt{public Account}}}::\mbox{{\texttt{transfer}}}({\mbox{{\texttt{dst}}}:\mbox{{\texttt{Account}}},\mbox{{\texttt{key'}}}:\mbox{{\texttt{Key}}},\mbox{{\texttt{amt}}}:\mbox{{\texttt{nat}}}})

\{\,a.\mbox{{\texttt{}}}=b\,\}\ {\,\parallel\,\ \{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}a.\mbox{{\texttt{}}}=b\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\}}

\{\,\mbox{{\texttt{this}}}\neq\mbox{{\texttt{dst}}}\wedge\mbox{{\texttt{this}}}.\mbox{{\texttt{}}}=b\wedge\mbox{{\texttt{dst}}}.\mbox{{\texttt{}}}=b^{\prime}\,\}\,

\ \ \ \ \ \ \mbox{{\texttt{public Account}}}::\mbox{{\texttt{transfer}}}({\mbox{{\texttt{dst}}}:\mbox{{\texttt{Account}}},\mbox{{\texttt{key'}}}:\mbox{{\texttt{Key}}},\mbox{{\texttt{amt}}}:\mbox{{\texttt{nat}}}})

E.1.
Examples of Semantics of our Specifications

Example E.3.

We revisit the specifications given in Sect.
2.1
, the three modules from Sect.
2.1.2
, and Example
E.2

\mbox{{\texttt{M}}}_{{\textit{good}}}\models S_{1}

\mbox{{\texttt{M}}}_{{\textit{good}}}\models S_{2}

\mbox{{\texttt{M}}}_{{\textit{good}}}\models S_{3}

\mbox{{\texttt{M}}}_{{\textit{good}}}\models S_{5}

\mbox{{\texttt{M}}}_{{\textit{bad}}}\models S_{1}

\mbox{{\texttt{M}}}_{{\textit{bad}}}\not\models S_{2}

\mbox{{\texttt{M}}}_{{\textit{bad}}}\not\models S_{3}

\mbox{{\texttt{M}}}_{{\textit{bad}}}\not\models S_{5}

\mbox{{\texttt{M}}}_{{\textit{fine}}}\models S_{1}

\mbox{{\texttt{M}}}_{{\textit{fine}}}\models S_{2}

\mbox{{\texttt{M}}}_{{\textit{fine}}}\not\models S_{3}

\mbox{{\texttt{M}}}_{{\textit{fine}}}\not\models S_{5}

Example E.4.

For Example
5.5
, we have

\mbox{{\texttt{M}}}_{{\textit{good}}}\models S_{7}
and
\mbox{{\texttt{M}}}_{{\textit{bad}}}\models S_{7}
and
\mbox{{\texttt{M}}}_{{\textit{fine}}}\models S_{7}
.
Also,
\mbox{{\texttt{M}}}_{{\textit{good}}}\models S_{8}
and
\mbox{{\texttt{M}}}_{{\textit{bad}}}\models S_{8}
and
\mbox{{\texttt{M}}}_{{\textit{fine}}}\models S_{8}
.
However,
\mbox{{\texttt{M}}}_{{\textit{good}}}\models S_{9}
, while
\mbox{{\texttt{M}}}_{{\textit{bad}}}\not\models S_{9}
.

Example E.5.

For any specification
S\triangleq{\{\,A\,\}\,p\ C\!::\!m(\overline{x:C})\,\{\,A^{\prime}\,\}}
and any module
M
which does not have a class
C
with a method
m
with formal parameter types
{\overline{C}}
, we have that
M\models S
.
Namely, if a method were to be called with that signature on a
C
from
M
, then execution would be stuck, and the requirements from Def.
5.4
(3) would be trivially satisfied.
Thus,
\mbox{{\texttt{M}}}_{{\textit{fine}}}\models S_{8}
.

E.1.1.
Free variables in well-formed specifications

We now discuss the requirements about free variables in well-formed specifications as defined in Def.
5.6
.
In scoped invariants,
A
may only mention variables introduced by the quantifier,
\overline{x}
. In method specifications, the precondition,
\overline{x:C^{\prime}}\wedge A
, may only mention the receiver,
this
, the formal parameters,
\overline{y}
, and the explicitly introduced variables,
\overline{x}
; it may
not
mention the result
res
. The postcondition,
A^{\prime}
, may mention these variables, and in addition, may mention the result,
res
. The mid-condition,
A^{\prime\prime}
is about a state which has at least one more frame than the current method’s,
and therefore it may not mention
this
, nor
\overline{y}
, nor
res
.

E.2.
Expressiveness

We argue the expressiveness of our approach by comparing with example specifications proposed in
(
Mackay et al. 2022
;
Devriese et al. 2016
;
Rao et al. 2023a
)
.

E.2.1.
The DOM

This is the motivating example in
(
Devriese et al. 2016
)
,
dealing with a tree of DOM nodes: Access to a DOM node
gives access to all its
parent
and
children
nodes, with the ability to
modify the node’s
property
– where
parent
,
children
and
property

are fields in class
Node
. Since the top nodes of the tree
usually contain privileged information, while the lower nodes contain
less crucial third-party information, we must be able to limit
access given to third parties to only the lower part of the DOM tree. We do this through a
Proxy
class, which has a field
node
pointing to a
Node
, and a field
height
, which restricts the range of
Node
s which may be modified through the use of the particular
Proxy
. Namely, when you hold a
Proxy
you can modify the
property
of all the descendants of the
height
-th ancestors of the
node
of that particular
Proxy
. We say that

pr
has
modification-capabilities
on
nd
, where
pr
is
a
Proxy
and
nd
is a
Node
, if the
pr.height
-th
parent

of the node at
pr.node
is an ancestor of
nd
.

We specify this property as follows:

\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ S_{dom\_1}\ \ \triangleq\ \ \ \rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{{$\mathbb{A}\!\!$}}{nd:\mbox{{\texttt{DomNode}}}}.{{\{}}{\ \forall pr:\mbox{{\texttt{Proxy}}}.[\ may\_modi\!f\!y(pr,nd)\rightarrow\langle\!\!\langle pr\rangle\!\!\rangle\ ]\ }{{\}}}

\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ S_{dom\_2}\ \ \triangleq\ \ \forall{nd:\mbox{{\texttt{DomNode}}},val:\mbox{{\texttt{PropertyValue}}}}
.

\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \{{\ \forall pr:\mbox{{\texttt{Proxy}}}.[\ may\_modi\!f\!y(pr,nd)\rightarrow\langle\!\!\langle pr\rangle\!\!\rangle\ ]\wedge nd.property=val}\ \}

where
may\_modi\!f\!y(pr,nd)\triangleq\exists k.[\ nd.parent^{k}=pr.node.parent^{pr.height}\ ]

Note that
S_{dom\_2}
is strictly stronger than
S_{dom\_1}

Mackay et al. 2022
specify this as:

⬇

DomSpec
states that the
property
of a node can only change if
some external object presently has
access to a node of the DOM tree, or to some
Proxy
with modification-capabilties
to the node that was modified.
The assertion
\exists{o}.[\ \mbox{{\texttt{o}}}:\mbox{{\texttt{extl}}}\wedge\langle\mbox{{\texttt{o}}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\texttt{access}}\ \mbox{{\texttt{pr}}}\rangle\ ]
is the contrapositive of our
\langle\!\!\langle pr\rangle\!\!\rangle
, but is is weaker than that, because it does not specify the frame from which
o
is accessible.
Therefore,
DOMSpec
is a stronger requirement than
S_{dom\_1}
.

E.2.2.
DAO

The Decentralized Autonomous Organization (DAO)
(
Christoph Jentsch 2016
)
is a well-known Ethereum contract allowing
participants to invest funds. The DAO famously was exploited with a re-entrancy bug in 2016,
and lost $50M. Here we provide specifications that would have secured the DAO against such a
bug.

\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ S_{dao\_1}\ \ \triangleq\ \ \ \rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{{$\mathbb{A}\!\!$}}{d:\mbox{{\texttt{DAO}}}}.{{\{}}{\ \forall p:\mbox{{\texttt{Participant}}}.[\ d.ether\geq d.balance(p)\ ]\ }{{\}}}

\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ S_{dao\_2}\ \ \triangleq\ \ \ \rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{{$\mathbb{A}\!\!$}}{d:\mbox{{\texttt{DAO}}}}.{{\{}}{\ \ d.ether\geq\sum_{p\in d.particiants}d.balance(p)\ \ }{{\}}}

The specifications above say the following:

\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ S_{edao\_1}

guarantees that the DAO holds more ether than the balance of any of its participant’s.

\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ S_{dao\_2}

guarantees that that the DAO holds more ether than the sum of the balances held by DAO’s participants.

S_{dao\_2}
is stronger than
S_{dao\_1}
. They would both have precluded the DAO bug. Note that these specifications do not mention capabilities.
They are, essentially, simple class invariants and could have been expressed with the techniques proposed already by
(
Meyer 1992a
)
.
The only difference is that
S_{dao\_1}
and
S_{dao\_2}
are two-state invariants, which means that we require that they are
preserved
,

i.e.
if they hold in one (observable) state they have to hold in all successor states,
while class invariants are one-state, which means they are required to hold in all (observable) states.

15
15

15

This should have been explained somewhere earlier.

We now compare with the specification given in
(
Mackay et al. 2022
)
.

DAOSpec1
in similar to
S_{dao\_1}
: iy
says that no participant’s balance may ever exceed the ether remaining
in DAO. It is, essentially, a one-state invariant.

⬇

1

\triangleq

:

\wedge

:

, similarly to

, in that it enforces a class invariant of
DAO
, something that could be enforced
by traditional specifications using class invariants.

(

)
gives one more specification:

⬇

DAOSpec2
states that if after some single step of execution, a participant’s balance is
m
, then

this occurred as a result of joining the DAO with an initial investment of
m
,

(b):

the balance is

and they’ve just withdrawn their funds, or

the balance was
m
to begin with

E.2.3.
ERC20

The ERC20
(
The Ethereum Wiki 2018
)
is a widely used token standard describing the basic functionality of any Ethereum-based token
contract.
This functionality includes issuing tokens, keeping track of tokens belonging to participants, and the
transfer of tokens between participants. Tokens may only be transferred if there are sufficient tokens in the
participant’s account, and if either they (using the
transfer
method) or someone authorised by the participant (using the
transferFrom
method) initiated the transfer.

For an
e:\mbox{{\texttt{ERC20}}}
, the term
e.balance(p)
indicates the number of tokens in participant
p
’s account at
e
.
The
assertion
e.allowed(p,p^{\prime})
expresses that participant
p
has been authorised to spend moneys from
p^{\prime}
’s account at
e
.

The security model in Solidity is not based on having access to a capability, but on who the caller of a method is.
Namely, Solidity supports the construct
sender
which indicates the identity of the caller.
Therefore, for Solidity, we adapt our approach in two significant ways:
we change the meaning of
\langle\!\!\langle\mathit{e}\rangle\!\!\rangle
to express that
\mathit{e}
did not make a method call.
Moreover, we introduce a new, slightly modified form of two state invariants of the form
\ \rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{{$\mathbb{A}\!\!$}}{\overline{x:C}}.{{\{}}{A}{{\}}}.{\{}{A^{\prime}}{{\}}}
which expresses that any execution which satisfies
A
, will preserve
A^{\prime}
.

We specify the guarantees of ERC20 as follows:

ç

\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ S_{erc\_2}\ \ \triangleq\ \ \ \rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{{$\mathbb{A}\!\!$}}{e:\mbox{{\texttt{ERC20}}},p,p^{\prime}:\mbox{{\texttt{Participant}}},n:\mathbb{N}}.\\
{\hskip 113.81102pt{{\{}}{\ \forall p^{\prime}.[\,(e.allowed(p^{\prime},p)\rightarrow\langle\!\!\langle p^{\prime}\rangle\!\!\rangle\,]\ }{{\}}}.{\{}{\ e.balance(b)=n\ }{{\}}}}

\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ S_{erc\_3}\ \ \triangleq\ \ \ \rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{{$\mathbb{A}\!\!$}}{e:\mbox{{\texttt{ERC20}}},p,p^{\prime}:\mbox{{\texttt{Participant}}}}.\\
{\hskip 113.81102pt{{\{}}{\ \forall p^{\prime}.[\,(e.allowed(p^{\prime},p)\rightarrow\langle\!\!\langle p^{\prime}\rangle\!\!\rangle\,]\ }{{\}}}.{\{}{\ \neg(e.allowed(p^{\prime\prime},p)\ }{{\}}}}

The specifications above say the following:

\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ S_{erc\_1}

guarantees that the the owner of an account is always authorized on that account – this specification is expressed using the original version of two-state invariants.

\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ S_{erc\_2}

guarantees that any execution which does not contain calls from a participant
p^{\prime}
authorized on
p
’s account will not affect the balance of
e
’s account. Namely, if the execution starts in a state in which
e.balance(b)=n
, it will lead to a state where
e.balance(b)=n
also holds.

\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ S_{erc\_3}

guarantees that any execution which does not contain calls from a participant
p^{\prime}
authorized on
p
’s account will not affect who else is authorized on that account. That is, if the execution starts in a state in which
\neg(e.allowed(p^{\prime\prime},p)
, it will lead to a state where
\neg(e.allowed(p^{\prime\prime},p)
also holds.

We compare with the specifications given in
(
Mackay et al. 2022
)
:
Firstly,
ERC20Spec1

says that if the balance of a participant’s account is ever reduced by some amount
m
, then
that must have occurred as a result of a call to the
transfer
method with amount
m
by the participant,
or the
transferFrom
method with the amount
m
by some other participant.

⬇

1

ERC20Spec1

\triangleq

from

e

:

ERC20

\wedge

e
.

(
p
)

=

m

+

m
’

\wedge

m

>

2

\exists

\langle\mbox{{\texttt{p'}}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\texttt{calls}}\ \mbox{{\texttt{e}}}.\mbox{{\texttt{transfer}}}(\mbox{{\texttt{p, m}}})\rangle

\vee

4

e.allowed(p,

p’
’)

\geq

m

\wedge

\langle\mbox{{\texttt{p''}}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\texttt{calls}}\ \mbox{{\texttt{e}}}.\mbox{{\texttt{transferFrom}}}(\mbox{{\texttt{p', m}}})\rangle
]’

Secondly,
ERC20Spec2
specifies under what circumstances some participant
p’
is authorized to
spend
m
tokens on behalf of
p
: either
p
approved
p’
,
p’
was previously authorized,
or
p’
was authorized for some amount
m + m’
, and spent
m’
.

⬇

from

ERC20

Object

ERC20Spec1
is related to
S_{erc\_2}
. Note that
ERC20Spec1
is more API-specific, as it expresses the precise methods which caused the modification of the balance.

E.2.4.
Wasm, Iris, and the stack

In
(
Rao et al. 2023a
)
, they consider inter-language safety for Wasm. They develop Iris-Wasm, a mechanized higher-order separation logic mechanized in Coq and the Iris framework. Using Iris-Wasm, with the aim to
specify and verify individual modules separately, and then compose them modularly in a simple host language
featuring the core operations of the WebAssembly JavaScript Interface. They develop a
logical relation that enforces robust safety: unknown, adversarial code can only affect other modules through
the functions that they explicitly export.
They do not offer however a logic to deal with the effects of external calls.

As a running example, they use a
stack
module, which is an array of values, and exports functions to inspect the stack contents or modify its contents.
Such a setting can be expressed in our language through a
stack
and a
modifier
capability.
Assuming a predicate
Contents(\mbox{{\texttt{stack}}},\mbox{{\texttt{i}}},\mbox{{\texttt{v}}})
, which expresses that the contents of
stack
at index
i
is
v
, we can specify the stack through

\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ S_{stack}\ \ \triangleq\ \ \ \rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{{$\mathbb{A}\!\!$}}{s:\mbox{{\texttt{Stack}}},i:\mathbb{N},\mbox{{\texttt{v}}}:\mbox{{\texttt{Value}}}}.{{\{}}{\ \langle\!\!\langle\mbox{{\texttt{s.modifier}}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\ \wedge\ Contents(\mbox{{\texttt{s}}},i,\mbox{{\texttt{v}}})\ }{{\}}}

In that work, they provide a tailor-made proof that indeed, when the stack makes an external call, passing only the inspect-capability, the contents will not change.
However, because the language is essentially functional, they do not consider the possibility that the external call might already have stored the modifier capability.
Moreover, the proof does not make use of a Hoare logic.

E.2.5.
Sealer-Unsealer pattern

The sealer-unsealer pattern, proposed by
Morris Jr. 1973
, is a security pattern to enforce data
abstraction while interoperating with untrusted code. He proposes a function

makeseal
which generating pairs of functions (
seal
,
unseal
), such that
seal
takes a value
v
and returns a low-integrity value
v^{\prime}
.
The function
unseal
when given
v^{\prime}
will return
v
. But there is no other way to obtain
v
out of
v^{\prime}
except throughthe use of the
usealer
.
Thus,
v^{\prime}
can securely be shared with untrusted code.
This pattern has been studied by
Swasey et al. 2017
.

We formulate this pattern here.
As we are working with an object oriented rather than a functional language, we assume the existence of a class

DynamicSealer
with two methods,
seal
, and
unseal
.
And we define a predicate
Sealed(v,v^{\prime},us)
to express that
v
has been sealed into
v^{\prime}
and can be unsealed using
us
.

Then, the scoped invariants

\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ S_{sealer\_1}\ \ \triangleq\ \ \ \rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{{$\mathbb{A}\!\!$}}{\mbox{{\texttt{v}}},\mbox{{\texttt{v}}}^{\prime},\mbox{{\texttt{us}}}:\mbox{{\texttt{Object}}}}.{{\{}}{\ \langle\!\!\langle\mbox{{\texttt{us}}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\ \wedge\ Sealed(\mbox{{\texttt{v}}},\mbox{{\texttt{v}}}^{\prime},\mbox{{\texttt{us}}})}{{\}}}

\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ S_{sealer\_2}\ \ \triangleq\ \ \ \rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{{$\mathbb{A}\!\!$}}{\mbox{{\texttt{v}}},\mbox{{\texttt{v}}}^{\prime},\mbox{{\texttt{us}}}:\mbox{{\texttt{Object}}}}.{{\{}}{\ \langle\!\!\langle\mbox{{\texttt{v}}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\ \wedge\ \langle\!\!\langle\mbox{{\texttt{us}}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\ \wedge\ Sealed(\mbox{{\texttt{v}}},\mbox{{\texttt{v}}}^{\prime},\mbox{{\texttt{us}}})}{{\}}}

expresses that the unsealer is not leaked to external code (
S_{sealer\_1}
), and that if the external world has no access to the high-integrity value
v
nor to the its unsealer
us
, then it will not get access to the value (
S_{sealer\_2}
).

Appendix F
Appendix to Section
6

F.1.
Preliminaries: Specification Lookup, Renamings, Underlying Hoare Logic

Definition
F.1
is broken down as follows:
S_{1}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny txt}}}}{{\leq}}}S_{2}
says that
S_{1}
is textually included in
S_{2}
;
S\thicksim S^{\prime}
says that
S
is a safe renaming of
S^{\prime}
;
{\vdash\ \ M\ \ \mathbf{:}\ \ S}
says that
S
is a safe renaming of one of the specifications given for
M
.

In particular, a safe renaming of
{\ \rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{{$\mathbb{A}\!\!$}}{\overline{x:C}}.{{\{}}{A}{{\}}}}
can replace any of the variables
\overline{x}
.
A safe renaming of
{\{\,A_{1}\,\}\,p\ D\!::\!m(\overline{y:D})\,\{\,A_{2}\,\}\,\,\parallel\,\,\{A_{3}\}}
can replace the formal parameters (
\overline{y}
) by actual parameters (
\overline{y^{\prime}}
) but requires the actual parameters not to include
this
, or
res
, (
i.e.

\mbox{{\texttt{this}}},\mbox{{\texttt{res}}}\notin\overline{y^{\prime}}
). – Moreover, it can replace the free variables which do not overlap with the formal parameters or the receiver (
\overline{x}={\hskip-1.0ptF\hskip-1.0pt}v(A_{1})\setminus\{{\overline{y}},\mbox{{\texttt{this}}}\}
).

Definition F.1.

For a module
M
and a specification
S
, we define:

•

S_{1}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny txt}}}}{{\leq}}}S_{2}\ \ \ \triangleq\ \ \ S_{1}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny txt}}}}{{=}}}S_{2}
, or
S_{2}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny txt}}}}{{=}}}S_{1}\wedge S_{3}
, or
S_{2}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny txt}}}}{{=}}}S_{3}\wedge S_{1}
, or
S_{2}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny txt}}}}{{=}}}S_{3}\wedge S_{1}\wedge S_{4}
for some
S_{3}
,
S_{4}
.

•

S\thicksim S^{\prime}
is defined by cases

–

{\ \rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{{$\mathbb{A}\!\!$}}{\overline{x:C}}.{{\{}}{A}{{\}}}}\thicksim{\ \rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{{$\mathbb{A}\!\!$}}{\overline{x^{\prime}:C}}.{{\{}}{A^{\prime}[\overline{x^{\prime}/x}]}{{\}}}}

–

{\{\,A_{1}\,\}\,p\ D\!::\!m(\overline{y:D})\,\{\,A_{2}\,\}\,\,\parallel\,\,\{A_{3}\}}\thicksim{\{\,A_{1}^{\prime}\,\}\,p\ D\!::\!m(\overline{y^{\prime}:D})\,\{\,A_{2}^{\prime}\,\}\,\,\parallel\,\,\{A_{3}^{\prime}\}}

\hskip 56.9055pt\ \ \triangleq\ \ \ A_{1}=A_{1}^{\prime}[\overline{y/y^{\prime}}][\overline{x/x^{\prime}}],\ \ A_{2}=A_{2}^{\prime}[\overline{y/y^{\prime}}][\overline{x/x^{\prime}}],\ \ A_{3}=A_{3}^{\prime}[\overline{y/y^{\prime}}][\overline{x/x^{\prime}}],\ \ \wedge

\hskip 56.9055pt\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \mbox{{\texttt{this}}},\mbox{{\texttt{res}}}\notin\overline{y^{\prime}},\ \ \overline{x}={\hskip-1.0ptF\hskip-1.0pt}v(A_{1})\setminus\{{\overline{y}},\mbox{{\texttt{this}}}\}

•

{\vdash\ \ M\ \ \mathbf{:}\ \ S}\ \ \ \triangleq\ \ \ \exists S^{\prime}.[\ \ S^{\prime}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny txt}}}}{{\leq}}}\mathscr{S}\!pec(M)\ \ \wedge\ \ S^{\prime}\thicksim S\ \ ]

The restriction on renamings of method specifications that the actual parameters should not to include
this
or
res

is necessary because
this
and
res
denote different objects from the point of the caller than from the point of the callee.
It means that we are not able to verify a method call whose actual parameters include
this
or
res
.
This is not a serious restriction: we can encode any such method call by preceding it with assignments to fresh local variables,
this’
:=
this
, and
res’
:=
res
, and using
this’
and
res’
in the call.

5.5
can be renamed as

{\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ S_{9r}}

\triangleq

\{\,a1:\mbox{{\texttt{Account}}},a2:\mbox{{\texttt{Account}}}\wedge\langle\!\!\langle a1\rangle\!\!\rangle\wedge\langle\!\!\langle a2.\mbox{{\texttt{key}}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\,\}\,

\ \ \ \ \ \ \mbox{{\texttt{public Account}}}::\mbox{{\texttt{set}}}({\mbox{{\texttt{nKey}}}:\mbox{{\texttt{Key}}}})

\{\,\langle\!\!\langle a1\rangle\!\!\rangle\wedge\langle\!\!\langle a2.\mbox{{\texttt{key}}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\,\}\ {\,\parallel\,\ \{{\langle\!\!\langle a1\rangle\!\!\rangle\wedge\langle\!\!\langle a2.\mbox{{\texttt{key}}}\rangle\!\!\rangle}\}}

{M\ \vdash\ \{\,A_{4}\,\}\,s\,\{\,A_{5}\,\}\,\,\parallel\,\,\{\,A_{6}\,\}}\hskip 39.83368ptM\vdash A_{1}\rightarrow A_{4}\hskip 39.83368pt{M\vdash A_{5}\rightarrow A_{2}}\hskip 39.83368pt{M\vdash A_{6}\rightarrow A_{3}}\\

F.4.
Extend the semantics and Hoare logic to accommodate scalars and conditionals

We extend the notion of protection to also allow it to apply to scalars.

Definition F.4 (Satisfaction of Assertions – Protected From).

extending the definition of Def

4.4
. We use
\alpha
to range over addresses,
\beta
to range over scalars, and
\gamma
to range over addresses or scalars.

We define
M,\sigma\models\langle\!\!\langle{\gamma}\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!{\gamma_{o}}
as:

(1)

M,\sigma\models\langle\!\!\langle{\alpha}\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!{\alpha_{o}}\ \ \ \triangleq

•

\alpha\neq\alpha_{0}
,
and

•

\forall n\in\mathbb{N}.\forall f_{1},...f_{n}..[\ \ \lfloor\alpha_{o}.f_{1}...f_{n}\rfloor_{\sigma}=\alpha\ \ \ \Longrightarrow\ \ \ M,\sigma\models{\lfloor\alpha_{o}.f_{1}...f_{n-1}\rfloor_{\sigma}}:C\ \wedge\ C\in M\ \ ]

(2)

M,\sigma\models\langle\!\!\langle{\gamma}\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!{\beta_{o}}\ \ \ \triangleq\ \ \ true

(3)

M,\sigma\models\langle\!\!\langle{\beta}\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!{\alpha_{o}}\ \ \ \triangleq\ \ \ false

(4)

M,\sigma\models\langle\!\!\langle{\mathit{e}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!{\mathit{e}_{o}}\ \ \ \triangleq

\exists\gamma,\gamma_{o}.[\ \ M,\sigma,{\mathit{e}}\hookrightarrow\gamma\ \wedge M,\sigma,{\mathit{e}_{0}}\hookrightarrow\gamma_{0}\ \wedge\ M,\sigma\models\langle\!\!\langle{\gamma}\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!{\gamma_{o}}\ \ ]

The definition from above gives rise to further cases of protection; we supplement the triples from
Fig.
7
with some further inference rules, given in Fig.
17
.
Namely, any expression
\mathit{e}
is protected from a scalar (rules
Prot-In
,
Prot-Bool
and
Prot-Str
).
Moreover, if starting at some
\mathit{e}_{o}
and following any sequence of fields
\overline{f}
we reach internal objects or scalars (
i.e.
never reach an external object), then any
\mathit{e}
is protected from
\mathit{e}_{o}
(rule
Prot_Intl
).

\frac{M\vdash\mathit{e}_{o}:\mbox{{\texttt{int}}}\rightarrow\langle\!\!\langle\mathit{e}\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!\mathit{e}_{o}}{}\quad[\textsc{Prot-Int}]\frac{M\vdash\mathit{e}_{o}:\mbox{{\texttt{bool}}}\rightarrow\langle\!\!\langle\mathit{e}\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!\mathit{e}_{o}}{}\quad[\textsc{Prot-Bool}]\frac{M\vdash\mathit{e}_{o}:\mbox{{\texttt{str}}}\rightarrow\langle\!\!\langle\mathit{e}\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!\mathit{e}_{o}}{}\quad[\textsc{Prot-Str}]{\frac{M\vdash\langle\!\!\langle\mathit{e}\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!\mathit{e}_{o}\wedge{\mathit{e}}\,:\mbox{{\texttt{intl}}}\rightarrow\mathit{e}\neq e^{\prime}}{}\quad[\textsc{Prot-Neq]}}{\frac{M\vdash A\rightarrow\forall\overline{f}.[\ \mathit{e}_{o}.\overline{f}\,:\mbox{{\texttt{intl}}}\vee\,\mathit{e}_{o}.\overline{f}:\mbox{{\texttt{int}}}\,\vee\,\mathit{e}_{o}.\overline{f}:\mbox{{\texttt{bool}}}\,\vee\,\mathit{e}_{o}.\overline{f}:\mbox{{\texttt{str}}}\ ]}{M\vdash A\rightarrow\langle\!\!\langle\mathit{e}\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!\mathit{e}_{o}}\quad[\textsc{Prot-Intl]}}

Figure 17
.
Protection for Scalar and Internal Types

Proof.

We first prove (L1):

We define
\sigma_{1}\triangleq(\overline{\phi_{1}},\chi)
, and
\sigma_{2}\triangleq(\overline{\phi_{2}}\cdot\phi,\chi)
.

The above definitions imply that:

(1)
\forall\alpha^{\prime},\forall\overline{f}.[\ \lfloor\alpha^{\prime}.\overline{f}\rfloor_{\sigma_{1}}=\lfloor\alpha^{\prime}.\overline{f}\rfloor_{\sigma_{2}}\ ]

(2)
\forall\alpha^{\prime}.[\ Rchbl(\alpha^{\prime},\sigma_{1})=Rchbl(\alpha^{\prime},\sigma_{2})\ ]

(3)
LocRchbl(\sigma_{2})=\bigcup_{\alpha^{\prime}\in Rng(\phi)}Rchbl(\alpha^{\prime},\sigma_{2})
.

We now assume that

(4)
M,\sigma_{1}\models\langle\!\!\langle\alpha\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!Rng(\phi)
.

and want to show that

(A?)
M,\sigma_{2}\models\langle\!\!\langle\alpha\rangle\!\!\rangle

From (4) and by definitions, we obtain that

(5)
\forall\alpha^{\prime}\in Rng(\phi).\forall\alpha^{\prime\prime}\in Rchbl(\alpha^{\prime},\sigma_{1}).\forall f.[\ M,\sigma_{1}\models\alpha^{\prime\prime}:\mbox{{\texttt{extl}}}\ \rightarrow\alpha^{\prime\prime}.f\neq\alpha\ ]
, and also

(6)
\alpha\notin Rng(\phi)

From (5) and (3) we obtain:

(7)
\forall\alpha^{\prime}\in LocRchbl(\sigma_{2}).\forall f.[\ M,\sigma_{1}\models\alpha^{\prime}:\mbox{{\texttt{extl}}}\ \rightarrow\alpha^{\prime}.f\neq\alpha\ ]

From (7) and (1) and (2) we obtain:

(8)
\forall\alpha^{\prime}\in LocRchbl(\sigma_{2}).\forall f.[\ M,\sigma_{2}\models\alpha^{\prime}:\mbox{{\texttt{extl}}}\ \rightarrow\alpha^{\prime}.f\neq\alpha\ ]

From (8), by definitions, we obtain

(10)
M,\sigma_{2}\models\langle\!\!\langle\alpha\rangle\!\!\rangle

which is (A?).

This completes the proof of (L1).

We now prove (L2):

We define
\sigma_{1}\triangleq(\overline{\phi_{1}}\cdot\phi,\chi)
, and
\sigma_{2}\triangleq(\overline{\phi_{2}},\chi)
.

The above definitions imply that:

(1)
\forall\alpha^{\prime},\forall\overline{f}.[\ \lfloor\alpha^{\prime}.\overline{f}\rfloor_{\sigma_{1}}=\lfloor\alpha^{\prime}.\overline{f}\rfloor_{\sigma_{2}}\ ]

(2)
\forall\alpha^{\prime}.[\ Rchbl(\alpha^{\prime},\sigma_{1})=Rchbl(\alpha^{\prime},\sigma_{2})\ ]

(3)
LocRchbl(\sigma_{1})=\bigcup_{\alpha^{\prime}\in Rng(\phi)}Rchbl(\alpha^{\prime},\sigma_{1})
.

We assume that

(4)
M,\sigma_{1}\models\langle\!\!\langle\alpha\rangle\!\!\rangle\wedge\mbox{{\texttt{extl}}}
.

and want to show that

(A?)
M,\sigma_{2}\models A\!-\!\!\!\,\!{\triangledown}Rng(\phi)
.

From (4), and unfolding the definitions, we obtain:

(5)
\forall\alpha^{\prime}\in LocRchbl(\sigma_{1}).\forall f.[\ M,\sigma_{1}\models\alpha^{\prime}:\mbox{{\texttt{extl}}}\ \rightarrow\alpha^{\prime}.f\neq\alpha\ ]
, and

(6)
\forall\alpha^{\prime}\in Rng(\phi).[\ \alpha^{\prime}\neq\alpha\ ]
.

From(5), and using (3) and (2) we obtain:

(7)
\forall\alpha^{\prime}\in Rng(\phi).\forall\alpha^{\prime\prime}\in Rchbl(\alpha^{\prime},\sigma_{2}).\forall f.[\ M,\sigma_{2}\models\alpha^{\prime\prime}:\mbox{{\texttt{extl}}}\ \rightarrow\alpha^{\prime\prime}.f\neq\alpha\ ]

From (5) and (7) and by definitions, we obtain

(8)
\forall\alpha^{\prime}\in Rng(\phi).[\ \models\alpha\langle\!\!\langle\alpha\rangle\!\!\rangle\!\!\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\--\!\!\!\!\!\!\times\ \ \!\!\alpha^{\prime}\ ]
.

From (8) and definitions we obtain (A?).

This completes the proof of (L2).

We now prove (L3):

We define
\sigma_{1}\triangleq(\overline{\phi_{1}}\cdot\phi_{1},\chi)
, and
\sigma_{2}\triangleq(\overline{\phi_{2}},\chi)
.

The above definitions imply that:

(1)
\forall\alpha^{\prime},\forall\overline{f}.[\ \lfloor\alpha^{\prime}.\overline{f}\rfloor_{\sigma_{1}}=\lfloor\alpha^{\prime}.\overline{f}\rfloor_{\sigma_{2}}\ ]

(2)
\forall\alpha^{\prime}.[\ Rchbl(\alpha^{\prime},\sigma_{1})=Rchbl(\alpha^{\prime},\sigma_{2})\ ]

.

, and

(A?)
M,\sigma_{2}\models A\!-\!\!\!\,\!{\triangledown}Rng(\phi)

.
